Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)

I. Executive Summary
1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
Under the 505(b)(1) regulatory pathway, Vertex Pharmaceuticals submitted new drug application
(NDA) 219209, suzetrigine (SUZ) for the treatment of moderate to severe acute pain in adults.
SUZ is a selective inhibitor of voltage-gated sodium channel subtype 1.8 (NaV1.8). These
channels are expressed in peripheral sensory neurons, including dorsal root ganglion (DRG)
neurons, and are believed to play a role in pain signaling.
The regulatory history is notable for Breakthrough Therapy designation and a Priority Review
determination. This NDA was not presented at the Anesthetic and Analgesic Drug Products
Advisory Committee because the benefit-risk assessment did not identify unexpected significant
safety or efficacy issues, and no controversial issues arose that would benefit from advisory
committee discussion.
Substantial evidence of effectiveness for SUZ in adults with moderate-to-severe acute pain was
established using data from two adequate and well-controlled trials. These studies show that SUZ
administered as 100 mg for the first dose, then 50 mg every 12 hours has significant reduction in
acute pain compared to placebo.
The safety database for SUZ is sufficient for the evaluation of risk. The available safety data
show that SUZ has a favorable safety profile and is safe for its intended use. Serious adverse
events were generally consistent with known postoperative complications of surgery and their
incidence was low and balanced between treatment arms. The most common adverse reactions
(greater incidence in SUZ-treated subjected compared to placebo) were pruritis, muscle spasms,
increased creatine phosphokinase, and rash. SUZ is associated with potentially significant drug
interactions and subjects with moderate hepatic impairment had greater SUZ and M6-SUZ
(active metabolite) exposure than those with normal hepatic function. Negative effects to fertility
and pregnancy were observed in nonclinical studies; however, the clinical relevance of these
findings is unclear.
This NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends
approval for adults with moderate-to-severe acute pain. The signatory authority concurs that
identified risks and uncertainties can be mitigated through labeling and further evaluated through
postmarketing studies and routine pharmacovigilance. The review team is requiring
postmarketing studies to evaluate the use of SUZ in pediatric patients and the risks to pregnancy
and lactation in females.
The overall benefit-risk is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework below (Table
2). For detailed information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the detailed
reviews included in this Integrated Assessment document and the Product Quality Review.

4
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)

1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with two or more adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigations.
The Applicant conducted two randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled phase 3
studies (Studies 104 and 105) using two different acute pain models to assess the efficacy of
SUZ. These studies provided sufficient evidence for the efficacy of SUZ for the treatment of
moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults.

Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)

2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
Dimension
Evidence and Uncertainties

Conclusions and Reasons

Acute pain is a common and well-recognized medical
condition that is nearly ubiquitous.
Moderate-to-severe acute pain is a serious clinical
condition that has a signficant impact on quality of life and
physical function.
Symptoms can last hours, days, or weeks.
There are numerous causes of acute pain. These include
surgery or other invasive procedures and nonsurgical
causes, such as trauma.
Inadequate pain management negatively impacts patients’
health and can increase the risk of developing chronic pain.
Multiple prescription and nonprescription medications are
used for the treatment of acute pain. A multimodal
approach involving the simultaneous use of different
analgesics is a commonly used strategy for treating acute
pain.
Pharmacologic options for the treatment of acute pain
include acetaminophen, NSAIDs, muscle relaxants, local
anesthetics, and opioids.
Opioids are often the treatment of choice for surgical and
nonsurgical causes of moderate-to-severe acute pain.
There are several significant risks associated with the use
of opioids, including respiratory depression, addiction,
overdose, and death.
The use of opioids can be limited by other side effects,
such as constipation, nausea, and vomiting.

6
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

Acute pain is a serious and common condition that is
associated with many different medical and surgical conditions.
Undertreated acute pain can result in significant medical and
economic consquences. There is an unmet need for novel
analgesics.

There are multiple medications currently used for the treatment
of acute pain. These can be used as individual therapies or in
conjunction with other analgesics. Acetaminophen and NSAIDs
are frequently used as first-line therapy for mild-to-moderate
pain, but their effectiveness can be limited for more severe
pain. Opioids are frequently used for moderate-to-severe acute
pain but are associated with multiple significant risks and side
effects.

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Dimension

Benefit

Evidence and Uncertainties
• Non-opioid analgesics can also be associated with
significant risks, such as NSAIDs and their potential effects
on the cardiovascular, gastrointestinal, and renal systems.
• The Applicant conducted two randomized, multicenter,
double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled phase 3
studies to assess the efficacy of SUZ for the treatment of
moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults. Both studies
evaluated a 100 mg loading dose followed by 50 mg every
12 hours for a 48-hour treatment period.
• The primary efficacy endpoint in both studies was the timeweighted sum of the pain intensity difference as recorded
on the NPRS from 0 to 48 hours (SPID48) compared to
placebo. The SPID48 is a cumulative assessment of pain
reduction from baseline.
• The key secondary efficacy endpoints in both studies were
SPID48 compared to HB/APAP and time to ≥2-point
reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo.
• Study 104 evaluated the efficacy of SUZ in a bunionectomy
pain model. In this study, SUZ demonstrated a statistically
greater reduction in pain than did placebo as measured by
the SPID48 with a least-squares (LS) mean difference of
29.3.
• Study 105 evaluated the efficacy of SUZ in an
abdominoplasty pain model. In this study, SUZ
demonstrated a statistically greater reduction in pain than
did placebo as measured by the SPID48 with a LS mean
difference of 48.4.
• SUZ did not demonstrate greater pain reduction than did
HB/APAP as measured by the SPID48 in either Study 104
or 105.

7
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

Conclusions and Reasons

Two placebo- and active-comparator (HB/APAP), adequate and
well-controlled phase 3 clinical studies were conducted using
two different acute pain models respresenting bony and soft
tissue pain. Compared to placebo, there is sufficient evidence
of efficacy of SUZ 100 mg followed by 50 mg every 12 hours.
These studies did not demonstrate superior efficacy of SUZ
compared to HB/APAP 5/325 mg every 6 hours. Considering
that this is a lower dosage regimen for HB/APAP, the use of
SUZ as a sole agent may be insufficient for the treatment of
more severe acute pain.

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Dimension
Risk and risk
management

Evidence and Uncertainties
• A total of 1883 unique subjects were exposed to at least 1
dose of SUZ (1266 with acute pain at the to-be-marketed
dose regimen in the phase 2 and 3 studies, 169 in the
acute pain studies who received a lower dose of SUZ, 393
in the phase 1 studies in healthy subjects, and 55 in the
phase 1 studies in special populations). The safety of SUZ
was evaluated in 874 subjects with acute postoperative
pain in controlled phase 3 studies 104 and 105 and 256
subjects in uncontrolled phase 3 study 107.
• There were no deaths in subjects treated with SUZ.
• In the controlled acute pain studies, the incidence of SAEs
and AEs leading to discontinuation of the study drug was
low overall, and there were no substantial differences
between the treatment arms.
• In the controlled acute pain studies, the most common AEs
(incidence >2%) were nausea, constipation, headache,
dizziness, and pruritis.
• There is no evidence of abuse potential with SUZ.
• Small changes in laboratory assessments of renal function
were noted, which were considered unlikely to be clinically
significant.
• There were negative effects on fertility and pregnancy
observed in nonclinical studies. However, there are
insufficient clinical data regarding the use of SUZ in
pregnancy and in patients of reproductive potential.

Conclusions and Reasons
SUZ represents a new pharamcologic class of non-opioid
therapy for acute pain. The size of the safety database was
adequate to characterize the safety profile of SUZ. The safety
profile was favorable in subjects with moderate-to-severe acute
pain and the known risks can be adequately described in
product labeling. There was no evidence of abuse potential.
Uncertainties surrounding the effects of SUZ on fertility,
pregnancy, and lactation will be addressed through labeling
and postmarketing studies.

Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; HB/APAP, hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen; LS, least-squares; NPRS, numeric pain rating scale; NSAID, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug;
SAE, serious adverse event; SPID48, time-weighted sum of the pain intensity difference as recorded on the NPRS from 0 to 48 hours; SUZ, suzetrigine

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
Moderate-to-severe acute pain is a serious and nearly ubiquitous condition that can result from a
wide variety of surgical and nonsurgical causes. Inadequately managed acute pain is significantly
detrimental to patients’ quality of life and is a risk factor for the development of chronic pain.
There are multiple pharmacologic classes available for the treatment of acute pain, including
acetaminophen, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs), and opioids. However,
significant limitations exist with currently available therapies, such as the abuse potential and
side effect profile of opioids and the limited effectiveness of non-opioids for severe pain.
Therefore, the treatment of acute pain continues to have unmet needs.
SUZ is a selective inhibitor of voltage-gated sodium channel subtype 1.8 (NaV1.8). This is
predominantly expressed in peripheral sensory neurons including DRG neurons. The Applicant
purported that this selectivity would lead to the avoidance of side effects that are associated with
less selective sodium channel modulators and central nervous system (CNS)-active agents.
The Applicant provided substantial evidence of efficacy of SUZ from two adequate and wellcontrolled studies using the bunionectomy and abdominoplasty acute pain models. The benefit of
SUZ is based on the time-weighted sum of the pain intensity difference as recorded on the
numeric pain rating scale (NPRS) from 0 to 48 hours (SPID48), which demonstrated statistically
significant pain relief compared to placebo in both studies. The first key secondary endpoint, a
comparison of SUZ versus an opioid active control (hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen
[HB/APAP] 5/325 mg every 6 hours) on SPID48, was not met in either study. In the study of
acute pain post-bunionectomy, subjects in the SUZ group had less pain reduction compared to
those in the HB/APAP group.
The safety evaluation of SUZ was adequate, and the demonstrated safety profile is acceptable for
the indicated dose and population. There were no concerning major safety findings and the
controlled phase 3 acute pain studies demonstrated that subjects treated with SUZ had a lower
incidence of some of the adverse events (AEs) commonly associated with opioids, such as
nausea, vomiting, and dizziness, than did those treated with HB/APAP. Additionally, the
incidence of any AE was lower in the SUZ group than those in either the HB/APAP or placebo
groups. SUZ does not appear to have a potential for abuse. SUZ is associated with potentially
significant drug interactions including some hormonal contraceptives; dosage modifications are
recommended for moderate CYP3A inhibitors and SUZ is contraindicated for use with strong
CYP3A inhibitors. Subjects with moderate hepatic impairment had greater SUZ and M6-SUZ
(active metabolite) exposure than those with normal hepatic function, and the recommended
dosage is lower in patients with moderate hepatic impairment than those with normal hepatic
function. Uncertainties regarding safety include use of SUZ in pediatric patients, patients with
severe hepatic impairment or renal impairment of eGFR < 15 mL/min, and the risks to fertility,
pregnancy, and lactation. These uncertainties will be addressed in the labeling and assessed
through postmarketing studies.
There is a clear unmet medical need for new therapies for the treatment of pain. SUZ is the first
member of a novel pharmacologic class of analgesics and has demonstrated efficacy for the
treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in two adequate and well-controlled clinical studies.
Considering the totality of the data, the benefits of SUZ clearly outweigh the risks. However, it is
important to note that the performance of SUZ in clinical studies compared to a HB/APAP
9
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
comparator administered at a relatively low dose may indicate limited efficacy as a sole agent for
the treatment of more severe acute pain. Nonetheless, its use as part of multimodal analgesic
therapy may reduce reliance on currently used analgesics and subsequently mitigate the
associated risks, such as abuse potential with opioid use. In summary, SUZ and the new
pharmacologic class it represents have the potential to be valuable additions to the current
armamentarium of analgesics.

Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Vertex Pharmaceuticals Inc. has developed suzetrigine (SUZ; VX-548), an inhibitor of voltagegated sodium channel 1.8 (NaV1.8). There are nine NaV isoforms expressed in humans, and SUZ
is selective for NaV1.8, which plays a role in pain signaling. SUZ is the first potential member of
this pharmacologic class of non-opioid analgesics. The Applicant asserts that because NaV1.8 is
selectively expressed in peripheral pain-sensing neurons in humans, NaV1.8 inhibitors are not
expected to have the abuse liability associated with opioids or the adverse effect profile observed
with central nervous system (CNS)-active agents.
The proposed indication for SUZ is for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults.
SUZ is an orally bioavailable small molecule, and the Applicant has developed a 50-mg tablet as
the to-be-marketed formulation. The proposed dosing regimen is 100 mg for the first dose
(b) (4)
(loading), followed by 50 mg every 12 hours
Pain is a nearly ubiquitous experience that impacts multiple facets of patients’ lives, including
physical functioning and mental health. There is a significant economic burden associated with
the management of pain. Acute pain is usually time-limited and can be associated with a variety
of surgical and nonsurgical conditions. Inadequately treated acute pain can increase the risk of
developing chronic pain. Moderate-to-severe acute pain is considered a serious clinical condition
for which there is an urgent need for safe and effective treatments.
There are multiple classes of FDA-approved medications for the management of acute pain in
adults. These include acetaminophen, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs), opioids,
and local anesthetics. Muscle relaxants are indicated as an adjunct for the treatment of
discomfort and muscle spasm associated with acute, painful musculoskeletal conditions.
However, these medications are all associated with various adverse effects across multiple
different organ systems. Opioids, in particular, have gained significant attention given their
abuse liability and the growing public health concerns regarding addiction and overdose.
Two phase 3 clinical studies (Studies 104 and 105) were conducted to assess the safety and
establish the efficacy of SUZ in moderate-to-severe acute pain. These studies were both
randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled in design. Study 104 used
bunionectomy and Study 105 used abdominoplasty as the postsurgical acute pain models. The
phase 3 program also included Study 107, a single-arm, uncontrolled study that evaluated SUZ in
various acute surgical and nonsurgical painful conditions for up to 14 days of treatment. In the
two controlled phase 3 studies, the primary endpoint was the time-weighted sum of the pain
intensity difference as recorded on the numeric pain rating scale (NPRS) from 0 to 48 hours
(SPID48) for SUZ compared to placebo.
SUZ was granted Breakthrough Therapy designation after review of data from its phase 2
clinical studies suggesting favorable efficacy compared to placebo, as well as a favorable clinical
safety profile and a mechanistic rationale for less abuse potential compared to opioids. This
NDA was granted Priority Review designation considering the potential for SUZ to be a
significant improvement in the safety of the treatment of acute pain.

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
The primary evidence for the efficacy and safety of SUZ for the proposed indication of the
treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults was provided by two adequate and wellcontrolled studies conducted in the United States (Studies 104 and 105). These were both
randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled phase 3 studies. The active comparator
in both studies was hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen, an opioid-containing combination
medication. In addition, a single-arm phase 3 study (Study 107) provided supportive safety data.
All of the phase 3 studies were conducted in accordance with good clinical practice (GCP) as
described in Integrated Addendum to International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) guideline
E6(R2) Integrated Addendum to ICH E6(R1): Guideline for Good Clinical Practice (November
2016).
Additional supportive safety information from the phase 2 acute pain studies (Studies 101 and
102) was reviewed. These were both randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, and activecontrolled studies conducted in the United States using the same acute pain models
(bunionectomy and abdominoplasty) as the controlled phase 3 studies. These studies evaluated
different dose regimens of SUZ in addition to the to-be-marketed regimen. This included 60 mg
for the first dose, then 30 mg q12h (Studies 101 and 102) and 20 mg for the first dose, then
10 mg q12h (Study 101).

4. Patient Experience Data
The Applicant submitted patient experience data to assess the efficacy of SUZ. The NPRS is a
patient-reported pain intensity measure that was collected at scheduled time points throughout
the controlled phase 3 studies. These measurements were used to produce the SPID48, which
was the primary efficacy endpoint for these studies. The verbal rating scale (VRS) is another
patient-reported pain intensity measure that was used as an inclusion criterion to ensure that all
subjects had moderate or severe pain at baseline in the controlled and uncontrolled phase 3
studies. The Patient Global Assessment (PGA) of study drug is a patient-reported measure of
overall pain control with the study drug that was also used in these studies. Safety monitoring in
Study 107 included the Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale, which is an assessment of
suicidal thoughts and behaviors.

Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
SUZ selectively inhibits voltage-gated sodium channel subtype 1.8 (NaV1.8), which is expressed
in peripheral sensory neurons, including dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neurons, among other
known voltage-gated sodium channel subtypes (NaV 1.1 through 1.9). The inhibitory activity of
SUZ for NaV 1.8 was evaluated in DRG neurons of human, monkey, rat, mouse, rabbit, and dog
in vitro. The half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) values of SUZ against human and
monkey NaV1.8 were 0.68nM and 0.75nM, respectively. SUZ was at least 80-fold less potent in
rodent NaV1.8 (IC50: 56nM) than in human NaV1.8. Therefore, in vivo primary pharmacology
studies were not conducted in rodents. A major human metabolite, M6-SUZ (also referred to as
VRT-1875420, M6), was also demonstrated to be a potent human NaV1.8 blocker with an IC50 of
2.5nM in in vitro assays using human DRG neurons.

18
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)

5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
SUZ is a new molecular entity with a mechanism of action of blocking the voltage-gated sodium channel NaV1.8 currents. This represents a new
(b) (4)
class of drugs, and there is currently no established pharmacologic class (EPC) for this class. The Applicant proposed
(b) (4)
as the preferred EPC; however, the review team did not consider this acceptable. The review team recommends “sodium channel
blocker” as an appropriate EPC for this class. Refer to Section 23 for a discussion on the rationale for selecting “sodium channel blocker” as the
appropriate EPC for SUZ.
Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics

Established
SUZ is a sodium channel blocker.
pharmacologic class

Mechanism of action
SUZ is a selective blocker of the NaV1.8 voltage-gated sodium channel compared to other known voltage-gated sodium
channels (i.e., NaV 1.1 through 1.9). NaV1.8 is predominantly expressed in peripheral sensory neurons, including dorsal root
ganglion (DRG) neurons, where its role is to transmit pain signals (action potentials). By selectively inhibiting NaV1.8
channels, SUZ inhibits transmission of pain signals to the spinal cord and brain. Based on an in vitro electrophysiology
assay in human DRG neurons, M6-SUZ is a less potent blocker of NaV1.8 than SUZ by 3.7fold.
Active moieties
SUZ and M6-SUZ are active moieties.
QT prolongation
At 2 times the exposure of the maximum recommended dose, clinically significant QTc prolongation was not observed

cumulative accuracy (% bias) was < ±15% and precision (%CV) was <15% for QC samples and calibration curves used the
LC-MS/MS bioanalytical assay. The results of incurred samples reanalysis were within acceptable limits.

Drug Information
The effective dosing regimen determined based on phase 2 dose ranging studies was 100 mg loading dose/50 mg q12h and
was studied in pivotal phase 3 trials
The maximum tolerated dose of SUZ has not been determined. In healthy adult subjects, the highest single dose evaluated
was 395 mg (Study 005 Part A) and the highest multiple dose and duration was 178 mg qd x 14 days (Study 005 Part B)
and 200 mg × 7 days (QT study- Study 009).
The single doses of SUZ were evaluated in the range of 7.5 mg to 395 mg. The AUC0-inf of SUZ and M6-SUZ increased
approximately dose proportionally up to and including 171 mg and increased less than dose proportionally at higher doses,
while Cmax of SUZ and M6-SUZ increased less than dose proportionally across all doses.
The PK after multiple doses of SUZ was evaluated up to 178 mg qd for 14 days. After multiple doses, both AUC0-24h and
Cmax of SUZ and M6-SUZ on Day 14 increased approximately dose proportionally through doses up to 65 mg and slightly
less than dose proportionally at higher doses.
The accumulation ratio based on simulations of a dose of 50 mg q12h for SUZ and M6-SUZ were 3.37 and 4.49,
respectively.
The concentrations near steady state (90% of steady state) are reached by Day 3 for SUZ and Day 5 for M6-SUZ and the
steady state is reached by Day 16 for SUZ and Day 21 for M6-SUZ.
The 50 mg to-be-marketed tablet formulation was evaluated in the phase 3 studies. The bridge between phase 2 formulation
(10-mg tablets) and to-be-marketed formulation (50-mg tablets) was via an in vivo relative BA study (Study 011).
Absorption
Absolute bioavailability of SUZ has not been determined.
SUZ: 3 hours (range: 1.5 to 5 hours); M6-SUZ: 10 hours (range: 4 to 48 hours)
Administration of SUZ 100 mg with a high-fat meal (800 to 1000 calories, 50% derived from fat), a moderate-fat meal (600
calories, 30% derived from fat) and a low-fat meal (up to 500 calories with no more than 25% derived from fat) results in
decreased initial concentrations of SUZ and M6-SUZ in comparison to a fasted state. The median Tmax of SUZ and M6-SUZ
in the fasted state is 3 hours and 8 to 10 hours, respectively. When administered in the fed state (a high-fat meal or a
moderate-fat meal), the median Tmax of SUZ was delayed to 5 hours and the median Tmax of M6-SUZ was delayed to 24
hours. The Cmax and AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ were not affected by any of the meal conditions including a high-fat meal
consumed 1 hour after the administration of SUZ. Administration of the second dose of 50 mg with or without regard to
meals is predicted to not affect the systemic exposures of SUZ and M6-SUZ.
Based on the observed decreased initial concentrations and delayed Tmax with both moderate- and high-fat meals in
comparison to a fasted condition, the labeling recommendation for food effect is, “Avoid consuming food from 2 hours before
until 1 hour after the first dose of JOURNAVX to avoid delay in onset of pain relief. Subsequent doses of JOURNAVX can be
taken with or without food.”

Drug Information
Distribution
Apparent volume of distribution based on the terminal phase (Vz/F) of SUZ is 495 L.
Plasma protein binding was 99% for SUZ and 96% for M6-SUZ. Both albumin and alpha-1 acid glycoprotein contribute to
SUZ and M6-SUZ binding in human plasma. In vitro blood-to-plasma concentration ratio was approximately 0.6 for both SUZ
and M6-SUZ in humans.
SUZ is not a substrate for transporters (P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1 or OATP1B3). M6-SUZ is a substrate for P-gp, but not other
transporters.
Elimination
Following [14C]SUZ oral administration, ~94% of the administered dose was recovered, out of which ~50% is recovered in
feces [primarily as metabolites, unchanged parent (9.1%) and unchanged M6-SUZ (<0.9%)] and ~44% in urine (as
metabolites)
The mean plasma apparent clearance is 13.9 L/h
Effective half-lives of SUZ and M6-SUZ are 24 hours and 33 hours, respectively.
SUZ and M6-SUZ are primarily metabolized by CYP3A, with a minor contribution from CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2C8.
SUZ was mainly cleared by oxidative metabolism.
After a single oral dose of [14C]SUZ, 44.0% and 49.9% of the dose is excreted in urine and feces, respectively.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight (44-126kg) did not have a clinically relevant impact on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ.
Age (18-75 years) does not have a clinically relevant impact on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ.
The PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ were not altered in patients with renal impairment of eGFR 15 to <60 mL/min (estimated by
CKD-EPI equation with adjustment for the body surface area). The effect of renal impairment (eGFR <15 mL/min) and
kidney failure not receiving dialysis on SUZ and M6-SUZ pharmacokinetics is unknown.
In patients with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class B), the Cmax and AUC0-12 SUZ were increased by 31% and
50%, respectively, and hence reduced dosing regimen of SUZ is needed in patients with moderate hepatic impairment
(Child-Pugh Class B). SUZ was not studied in subjects with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class C) and should not
be used in that population.

Drug Information
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
SUZ inhibits CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19, but is not expected to result in clinically significant drug interactions. SUZ
does not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2D6, and CYP3A enzymes, and M6-SUZ does not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6,
CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A enzymes.
SUZ is CYP3A inducer in vitro. SUZ decreased the AUC of midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A substrate) by 48% and Cmax by
37% (Study 010). Since SUZ decreases the exposure of CYP3A substrates and may compromise the efficacy, the review
team included labeling recommendations for certain CYP3A substrates (refer to section 8.2.2). Compared to CYP3A, SUZ
has lesser potential to induce CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19. SUZ does not induce CYP1A2.

Inhibition/induction of
transporter systems

Inhibitors and inducers of
metabolism

Inhibitors/inducers of
transporter systems

SUZ has no significant effect on the PK of levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol and therefore, SUZ can be used with oral
contraceptives containing levonorgestrel, norethindrone and ethinyl estradiol.
SUZ is an inhibitor of P-gp in vitro. However, a clinical DDI study (010) showed that SUZ did not affect the PK of digoxin (Pgp substrate). No dosage adjustment of SUZ is needed when administered with P-gp substrates. SUZ and M6-SUZ inhibit
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT3 but is not expected to result in clinically significant drug interactions. SUZ and M6-SUZ does
not inhibit BCRP OAT1, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2/K transporters.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Substrate)
SUZ and M6-SUZ are CYP3A substrates.
Itraconazole, a strong CYP3A inhibitor increased the AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 4.79-fold and 4.42-fold, respectively
(Study 008). Therefore, concomitant use with strong CYP3A inhibitors is contraindicated. When SUZ is concomitantly used
with moderate CYP3A inhibitors, a dosage reduction of SUZ is recommended.
Rifampin, a strong CYP3A inducer, decreased AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 93% and 85%, respectively, and decreased the
Cmax of SUZ by 80% and increased that of M6-SUZ by 30% (Study 013). Avoid concomitant use of SUZ with strong and
moderate CYP3A inducers.
SUZ and M6-SUZ are not substrates of BCRP, OATP1B1 or OATP1B3. SUZ is not a P-gp substrate, but M6-SUZ is a P-gp
substrate.

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
Dosing regimen selection for the acute pain indication of SUZ is based on two phase 2
randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled dose-ranging studies in bunionectomy
(Study 101) and abdominoplasty (Study 102). Study 101 evaluated the efficacy of three dosing
regimens: 1) 100-mg loading dose (LD)/50 mg q12h for 36 hours, 2) 60-mg LD/30 mg q12h for
36 hours, and 3) 20-mg LD/10 mg q12h for 36 hours, while Study 102 evaluated two dosing
regimens: 1) 100-mg LD/50 mg q12h for 36 hours, and 2) 60-mg LD/30 mg q12h for 36 hours.
Regimens were evaluated against an active comparator, hydrocodone/acetaminophen 5/325 mg
q6h for 36 hours, and against placebo. In each trial, pain intensity was measured using a patientreported 11-point NPRS, ranging from 0 to 10, where 0 corresponded to no pain and 10
corresponded to the worst pain imaginable. The efficacy of SUZ was evaluated by SPID48
compared to placebo and HB/APAP. The primary efficacy criteria of SPID48 for SUZ compared
to placebo was met at the highest dosing regimen, 100 mg/50 mg dosed at q12h for 36 hours.
The magnitude of effect in the SUZ group with the highest dosing regimen was greater than
those of the lower-dose groups, and no SUZ dose group other than the highest dose showed a
statistically significant difference from placebo. Therefore, the highest dosing regimen of
100-mg LD/50 mg q12h was utilized in the pivotal phase 3 trials in the same pain models of
bunionectomy and abdominoplasty. In both phase 3 trials, treatment with SUZ demonstrated
statistically significant superior reduction in pain compared to placebo.

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
6.2.1. Study VX22-548-104 (Study 104)
6.2.1.1. Design, Study 104
Study 104 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, three-arm, parallel-group study
evaluating the efficacy and safety of SUZ in treating acute pain after a bunionectomy. The
primary efficacy endpoint was the time-weighted SPID48 compared to placebo. The key
secondary efficacy endpoints included SPID48 compared to HB/APAP and time to ≥2-point
reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo. Additional design information is
available in Section 15.1.
As described in the draft guidance for industry Development of Non-Opioid Analgesics for Acute
Pain (February 2022), the randomized, double-blind, repeat-dose design of this superiority study
with a treatment duration of 48 hours is acceptable to support a finding of efficacy for a nonopioid analgesic. The primary outcome measure of NPRS is a patient-reported measure of pain
23
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
intensity that is well defined and reliable. The primary endpoint of sum of the pain intensity
difference (SPID) over the prespecified treatment period is an acceptable analysis for an
analgesic intended for acute pain. The time to onset of SUZ as an acute analgesic was evaluated
in an acceptable manner using the times to ≥2-point and ≥1-point reduction in NPRS from
baseline compared to placebo, as discussed at the July 14, 2022, end-of-phase 2 meeting.
6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study 104
Males and females 18 to 80 years of age, inclusive, scheduled to undergo a primary unilateral
bunionectomy with distal first metatarsal osteotomy (i.e., Austin procedure) and internal fixation
under regional anesthesia (Mayo and popliteal sciatic block) were eligible for this study. After
surgery, subjects must have reported pain that was moderate or severe on the VRS and ≥4 on the
NPRS within 9 hours after removal of the popliteal sciatic block on Day 1.
Subjects who had a prior history of bunionectomy or other foot surgery on the index foot, or
bunionectomy on the opposite foot as part of this study, were excluded. Subjects who had
chronic use of opioids or NSAIDs with dose escalation within 30 days before admission or were
unwilling or unable to stop analgesics at least 5 half-lives or 2 days (whichever was longer)
before admission were also excluded.
See Section 15.1 for other inclusion and exclusion criteria.
6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study 104
Study 104 assumed a standardized effect size of 0.4 for SUZ compared to placebo and 0.25 for
SUZ compared to HB/APAP; 338 evaluable subjects per group for SUZ and HB/APAP and 169
evaluable subjects for placebo would provide more than 90% power for the primary endpoint of
SUZ versus placebo on SPID48 and 90% power for the first key secondary endpoint of SUZ
versus HB/APAP on SPID48. These calculations were based on two-sample t-tests at a two-sided
significance level of 0.05. To account for a 15% dropout rate, a total of approximately 1,000
subjects were planned to be enrolled in Study 104.
The treatment effects assumed in the sample size planning for Study 104 appeared to be
conservative as compared to the treatment effects estimated from the previously completed phase
2 study for bunionectomy surgical model; the estimated standardized effect size was 0.85 for
SUZ versus placebo on SPID48 and 0.69 for SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48.
Analysis Sets
The all subjects set included all subjects who had been randomized or had received at least one
dose of the study drug. This analysis set was used for subject listings and the disposition
summary table.
The intent-to-treat (ITT) set included all randomized subjects. Subjects in the ITT set were
analyzed according to their randomized treatment group.
The full analysis set (FAS) included all randomized subjects who had received at least one dose
of study drug. Subjects in the FAS were analyzed according to their randomized treatment group.
The FAS was used for summaries of subject demographics and baseline characteristics, and all
efficacy analyses.
24
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
The safety set included all subjects who received at least one dose of study drug. Subjects in the
safety set were analyzed according to the treatment they received. The safety set was used for all
safety analyses.
Multiplicity Adjustment
A hierarchical testing procedure was used to control the overall type I error rate at a two-sided
alpha level of 0.05 accounting for all the tests planned for the primary and key secondary
endpoints. The testing order was as follows:
1. Primary endpoint: SUZ versus placebo on SPID48
2. First key secondary endpoint: SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48
3. Second key secondary endpoint: SUZ versus placebo on time to a ≥2-point reduction in
NPRS from baseline
Analysis of Primary Endpoint: SUZ Versus Placebo on SPID48
The Applicant defined pain intensity difference as the difference of NPRS scores between the
baseline and a given time point. SPID was calculated as ∑(𝑡𝑖―1 ― 𝑡𝑖) × 𝑃𝐼𝐷𝑡𝑖 , where 𝑡𝑖 was the
scheduled time since first dose (in hours) for time point 𝑖, and 𝑃𝐼𝐷𝑡𝑖 was the NPRS at time point
𝑖 minus NPRS at baseline. The primary endpoint was SPID48.
The primary analysis of SPID48 was based on the analysis of covariance model, which included
SPID48 as the dependent variable, treatment arm (SUZ, HB/APAP, or placebo) as a fixed effect,
and site and baseline NPRS as covariates. The treatment effect was estimated by the leastsquares (LS) mean difference between SUZ and placebo at a two-sided alpha level of 0.05. The
LS mean and its 95% confidence interval (CI) were provided for both the group comparison and
each treatment group.
The Applicant used the following imputation methods under four different conditions:
1. Rescue medication use: A fixed imputation window of 6 hours was prespecified for
ibuprofen. The prerescue NPRS score was collected immediately before the administration of
rescue medication. The previous NPRS score was used if the prerescue score was missing,
provided that the previous NPRS score did not include any observed score from the previous
rescue period. In case of multiple administrations of rescue medication that led to
(b) (4)
overlapping rescue periods, the prerescue NPRS score
replaced all
(b) (4)
NPRS scores
through the end of the last overlapping
rescue period.
2. Intercurrent event (ICE) of treatment discontinuation: The single imputation method of
baseline observation carried forward (BOCF) was used for the treatment discontinuation due
to an adverse event (AE). The single imputation method of last observation carried forward
(LOCF) was used for treatment discontinuation due to all other reasons than AE.
3. Subject who completed the treatment but with missing data from a certain time point to hour
48: A single imputation method of LOCF was used to impute missing data.
4. Intermittently missing NPRS scores: Linear interpolation was used to impute intermittent
missing data.
25
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Sensitivity Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
The Applicant planned and conducted two sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint. In the
first sensitivity analysis, the last NPRS score prior to discontinuation was used to impute the
missing NPRS scores following treatment discontinuation due to an AE. In the second sensitivity
analysis, the same primary analysis was performed using the ITT Set. Randomized subjects who
did not receive any study drug were assigned a value of 0 for the primary endpoint of SPID48.
Analysis of the Key Secondary Endpoints
For the first key secondary endpoint of SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48, the Applicant used
the same imputation methods as described for the primary endpoint.
The second key secondary endpoint, time to ≥2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline
compared to placebo, was defined as the time elapsed from the first dose of the study drug until
the first time the subject had at least a 2-point reduction in the NPRS from baseline. A KaplanMeier method was used to estimate the median time and the survival curve for each treatment
group, and a log-rank test was used to compare the survival curves between the treatment groups.
The median time with 95% CI from the Kaplan-Meier analysis and the p-value for the group
comparison from the log-rank test were reported.
Subgroup Analyses
Subgroup analyses were performed for sex, age, and race.
6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, Study 104

The demographic and baseline characteristics of the FAS for Study 104 are shown in Table 7.
Percentages were calculated using the number of participants in each treatment group as the
denominator. The three treatment groups were generally balanced on demographics and baseline
characteristics. Overall, the study population was predominantly female (85%), White (71%) or
Black or African American (24%), and aged 18 to 65 (91%).

Subject Disposition
In Study 104, a total of 1,075 subjects were randomized. There were two subjects who were
randomized but not dosed, resulting in a total of 1,073 subjects in the FAS. Among them,
938 (87.4%) completed treatment. The most common reason for discontinuation of treatment
was lack of efficacy (11.2%). The SUZ group had fewer discontinuations due to lack of efficacy
(12.0%) than did the placebo group (16.2%) but had more than the HB/APAP group did (7.9%).
One subject in the HB/APAP group discontinued due to a pretreatment AE. Table 8 shows
subject disposition.

Protocol Deviations
An important protocol deviation (IPD) was defined as any protocol deviation that may have
significantly affected the completeness, accuracy, and/or reliability of the study data or one that
may have significantly affected a subject’s rights, safety, or well-being.
A total of 54 (5.0%) subjects (17 subjects in SUZ, 22 subjects in HB/APAP, and 15 subjects in
placebo) had one or more IPDs. Among all the IPDs, the most common protocol deviations were
related to the study conduct/procedures (18 subjects, 1.7%). The next most common deviations
were related to the randomization criteria (15 subjects, 1.4%), followed by deviations related to
the eligibility criteria (14 subjects, 1.3%).
In general, the number of major protocol deviations that could potentially impact the efficacy
analysis was small. The frequency of minor protocol deviations was similar across the three
treatment groups. The minor protocol deviations that occurred were not anticipated to have a
measurable effect on the efficacy analysis.

summarizes the rescue medication use from 0 to 48 hours by treatment group. The mean total
usage was 1,009.3 mg in the placebo group and 862.9 mg in the SUZ group. Table 10 below
stratifies the rescue medication use by treatment group over time. The SUZ group had a similar
proportion of subjects using rescue medication (69.5%) compared to the placebo group (69.4%)
in the first 6 hours. The proportion of subjects using rescue medication in the SUZ and placebo

Efficacy Results - Secondary Endpoints
The analysis results of the first key secondary endpoint of SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48 are
shown in Table 12. The Applicant conducted a two-sided test for comparing SUZ to HB/APAP
on SPID48, which resulted in a p-value of 0.0016, indicating that there was a statistically
significant difference between SUZ and HB/APAP in terms of SPID48. The LS mean difference
calculated as SPID48 in SUZ minus SPID48 in HB/APAP, where the HB/APAP group was
treated as the reference group, was estimated to be −20.2 (95% CI: −32.7, −7.7), further
indicating that subjects in the SUZ group had less pain reduction than did those in the HB/APAP
group. Above mentioned results indicate that the Applicant failed to demonstrate superiority of
SUZ over HB/APAP on SPID48.

6.2.2. Study VX22-548-105 (Study 105)
6.2.2.1. Design, Study 105
Study 105 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, three-arm, parallel-group study
evaluating the efficacy and safety of SUZ in treating acute pain after an abdominoplasty. The
primary efficacy endpoint was the SPID48 compared to placebo. The key secondary efficacy
endpoints included SPID48 compared to HB/APAP and time to ≥2-point reduction in NPRS
from baseline compared to placebo. The overall study design was consistent with that of Study
104. The major differences were study procedures related to abdominoplasty, the anesthetic used
for surgery, and procedure-specific subject selection criteria. As with Study 104, the design of
this superiority study with a treatment duration of 48 hours was acceptable to support a finding
of efficacy for a non-opioid analgesic. Additional design information is available in Section 14.7.
6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study 105
Males and females 18 to 80 years of age, inclusive, scheduled to undergo a standard (“full”)
abdominoplasty procedure were eligible for this study. Additional inclusion criteria regarding the
procedure consisted of the following: the procedure must have included a horizontally oriented
incision approximately extending to each anterior superior iliac spine, umbilical dissection and
relocation, and plication of the fascia of the rectus muscle above and/or below the umbilical
stalk; may have included drain placement and skin closure at the discretion of the surgeon; and
must NOT have included a vertically oriented supraumbilical incision or collateral procedures
(e.g., liposuction). After surgery, subjects must have reported pain that was moderate or severe
on the VRS and ≥4 on the NPRS within 4 hours after surgery completion on Day 1. In addition,
31
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
the abdominoplasty procedure duration must have been ≤3 hours. Lastly, all analgesic guidelines
must have been followed during and after abdominoplasty including the use of intravenous
general anesthesia along with infiltration of 1% lidocaine without epinephrine at the surgical site
after completing the rectus plication.
Subjects who had a prior history of abdominoplasty were excluded. Subjects who had a history
of intra-abdominal and/or pelvic surgery that resulted in any complications or, in the opinion of
the investigator or medical monitor, would preclude participation in the study were also
excluded. The eligibility criteria were otherwise generally consistent with those of Study 104.
See Section 15.2 for other inclusion and exclusion criteria.
6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study 105
The sample size planning for Study 105 was identical to that for Study 104. The treatment effects
assumed in the sample size planning for Study 105 were also conservative as compared to the
treatment effects estimated from the previously completed phase 2 study in the abdominoplasty
surgical model; the estimated standardized effect size was 0.88 for SUZ versus placebo on
SPID48, and 0.78 for SUZ versus HB/APAP on SPID48.
Since the statistical approaches used in Study 105 were identical to those used in Study 104,
refer to Section 6.2.1.3 for details of the statistical analysis plan (SAP).
6.2.2.4. Results of Analyses, Study 105
Demographics and Clinical Characteristics
The demographic and baseline characteristics of the FAS for Study 105 are shown in Table 13.
Percentages were calculated using the number of participants in each treatment group as the
denominator. The three treatment groups were generally balanced on demographics and baseline
characteristics. Overall, the study population was predominantly female (98%), White (70%) or
Black or African American (27%), and aged 18 to 65 (>99%).

Protocol Deviations
A total of 49 (4.4%) subjects (19 subjects in SUZ, 17 subjects in HB/APAP, and 13 subjects in
placebo) had one or more IPDs. Among those IPDs, the most common protocol deviations were
related to the eligibility criteria (26 subjects, 2.3%). The next most common deviations were
related to the informed consent (10 subjects, 0.9%), followed by deviations related to the
randomization criteria (9 subjects, 0.8%).

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Potential Differences in Efficacy Between Pain
Models
Issue
Is there a major difference in the efficacy of SUZ between the bony and soft tissue pain models
evaluated in the phase 3 acute pain studies?
Background
The results from two randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled phase 3 studies
were used to demonstrate the efficacy of SUZ in treating moderate to severe acute pain. Two
established acute pain models were utilized: bunionectomy for bony tissue pain (Study 104) and
abdominoplasty for soft tissue pain (Study 105). Though each study was convincing on its own
to establish effectiveness, differences in the time course of pain relief were observed suggesting
that the effect of SUZ on acute pain could be pain model dependent. Specifically, in the
abdominoplasty study the SUZ group demonstrated a greater mean pain intensity difference
(PID) compared to placebo with sustained separation between the groups at all evaluated time
points (i.e., 0 to 48 hours). For the bunionectomy study, the SUZ group also demonstrated a
greater mean PID compared to placebo; however, the separation between pain curves was only
sustained from 8 through 48 hours. To evaluate this difference in the time course of pain relief

37
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
and the potential cause(s), perioperative pain management, baseline characteristics, and rescue
medication use were assessed between Study 104 and Study 105.
Perioperative Pain Management
In Study 104, the bunionectomy was performed under regional anesthesia (Mayo and popliteal
sciatic block) with intravenous sedation. Postoperatively, a continuous popliteal sciatic block
infusion (0.2% ropivacaine) was initiated and remained in place until approximately 3 a.m., but
no later than 5 a.m., on post-operative Day 1. Subjects were eligible for the study if they met the
VRS and NPRS criteria within 9 hours after removal of the popliteal sciatic infusion.
Ropivacaine boluses and/or infusion rate changes were permitted to be used as needed prior to
removal of the popliteal block for subjects with an NPRS score of ≥4; however, boluses were not
to be used at the time of block removal.
In Study 105, the abdominoplasty was performed under intravenous general anesthesia with
fentanyl citrate and propofol, along with infiltration of 1% lidocaine at the surgical site, after
completing the rectus plication. The rectus plication is typically performed toward the end of the
abdominoplasty and involves suturing separated abdominal muscles together to restore
abdominal strength. Subjects were eligible for the study if they met the VRS and NPRS criteria
within 4 hours after surgery completion.
Baseline Characteristics
Baseline characteristics were compared between Study 104 and 105. Refer to the Tables of
Baseline Demographics for Studies 104 and 105 in Section 6.2. While the baseline
characteristics were similar between the SUZ and placebo groups within each study, there were
differences in baseline characteristics between Study 104 and 105. Overall, the SUZ group in
Study 104 (bunionectomy) as compared to Study 105 (abdominoplasty) enrolled a decreased
percentage of women (85.9 vs. 97.8), subjects of increased age (median: 49 vs. 41 years), and
lower BMI (median 27.77 vs. 29 kg/m2). As noted in the excerpted tables below, though the
median NPRS scores were similar between Study 104 and 105, the mean NPRS was lower in
Study 104 (6.7 vs. 7.3 in Study 105) and a lower percentage of subjects reported severe pain on
the VRS in Study 104 (31.7 vs. 40.5 in Study 105).

Rescue Medication Use
Study 104 and 105 used ibuprofen 400 mg orally q6h as needed for rescue medication. Table 21
and Table 22 are reproduced below from sections 6.2.1.4 and 6.2.2.4, respectively, so that the
rescue medication use can be compared between the SUZ and placebo groups in Studies 104 and
105. The percentage of subjects using rescue medication from 0 to 6 hours was of greatest
interest in assessing the potential cause(s) of differences in the time course of pain relief (i.e.,
separation or non-separation of pain curves). Table 21 shows that the percentage of subjects
using rescue medication in Study 104 (bunionectomy) was nearly identical between the placebo
and SUZ groups during the 0- to 6-hour time block (69.4% vs. 69.5%) as well as throughout the
study. Table 22 shows that the percentage of subjects using rescue medication in Study 105
(abdominoplasty) was higher in the placebo group compared to the SUZ group for the 0- to 6hour time block (82.4% vs. 64.5%) as well as at 48 hours.

Assessment
The study populations enrolled in Study 104 and Study 105 had some differences in baseline
demographics and clinical characteristics, including a difference in mean NPRS between Study
104 and Study 105 (6.7 vs. 7.3). However, it is unlikely that these accounted for the differences
in the time course of pain relief between the two studies (i.e., the lack of separation of the pain
curves from 0 to 8 hours in Study 104 as compared to Study 105). Additionally, Studies 104 and
40
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
105 were identical with respect to study drug administration, endpoints assessed, and statistical
analysis.
Moreover, the rescue medication use in Study 104 was evaluated to determine if significant
differences in usage between the SUZ and placebo groups accounted for the lack of separation of
the pain curves in that study. Table 21 showed that the percentage of subjects using rescue
medication use was nearly identical between the SUZ and placebo groups from 0 to 6 hours and
remained similar for the duration of the treatment period (i.e., through 48 hours). Therefore,
rescue medication use does not appear to account for the lack of separation of pain curves in
Study 104.
Lastly, there were significant differences in perioperative pain management between Study 104
(bunionectomy) and Study 105 (abdominoplasty). As noted above, Study 104 was performed
under regional anesthesia with ropivacaine, both as the nerve block and then as a postoperative
nerve block infusion. Notably, ropivacaine is a non-selective sodium channel blocker which
targets voltage-gated sodium channels, including Nav1.8. In contrast, Study 105 used
intravenous general anesthesia, which does not act on sodium channels, during the procedure.
However, lidocaine, a non-selective sodium channel blocker, was administered for localized
anesthesia toward the end of the abdominoplasty procedure while performing the rectus
plication. Therefore, the duration of action of ropivacaine and lidocaine, and the overlap of the
duration of action of each drug with the first administration of SUZ was critical to understanding
the differences in pain curves between these two surgical pain models.
A nerve block with ropivacaine, as in Study 104, typically provides analgesia for at least 4-8
hours, and catheter placement extends the duration of analgesia. By contrast, analgesia provided
by local infiltration with lidocaine, as performed in Study 105, typically lasts last for 1-2 hours.
Given this duration of action and the overlap with the qualifying time period for first
administration of SUZ, it is plausible that for Study 104, ropivacaine accounted for lack of
separation of the pain curves between SUZ and placebo. Patients became eligible for the first
dose of SUZ as soon as the ropivacaine infusion was discontinued and within the subsequent 9
hours. Therefore, there is significant overlap between the potential duration of action of
ropivacaine and the first dose of SUZ. In comparison, for Study 105, the local infiltration of
lidocaine is administered in advance of closure of the surgical wound, and therefore, despite an
approximately 2-hour duration of action, the window of overlap with the qualifying SUZ
administration period is much shorter. Therefore, the differences in perioperative pain
management between the two studies, including the mechanism of action and the duration of
action of those drugs provides a plausible explanation that may account for the difference in time
course and degree of pain relief in Study 104.
Conclusion
Study 104 and Study 105 are adequate and well-controlled studies, each convincing on its own to
establish effectiveness. The difference in time course of pain relief between the two studies may
reflect the differences in perioperative pain management between the bunionectomy and
abdominoplasty pain models.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
The first versions of the SAPs for Studies 104 and 105 were submitted on April 14, 2023. In the
SAPs, the Applicant proposed to impute for subjects who discontinued the treatment due to lack
of efficacy (LOE) or an AE using the last NPRS score prior to discontinuation.
The Agency provided comments regarding the imputation method to be used for ICEs of
treatment discontinuation in an advice letter dated September 5, 2023. The Applicant responded
to the comments and provided the following justifications to support their proposal: “Imputation
using the last score does not attribute favorable pain scores to subjects who discontinue treatment
due to lack of efficacy in acute pain studies.”
The evidence to support this conclusion was their phase 2 study results where the pooled mean
baseline NPRS scores was lower than the pooled mean last NPRS scores for Studies 101 and
102, which correspond to the two phase 3 Studies 104 and 105. The Applicant further stated,
“However, imputation using the baseline score assumes subjects will have some improvement in
pain if they hadn’t discontinued treatment due to lack of efficacy, which is not appropriate.”
The Agency provided the following follow-up comment in response to the Applicant’s
justification:
The Division acknowledges your proposal to lock the database for the VX22-548-105
Study. We have provided our comments in September 5, 2023 advice letter in response to
your April 14, 2023 submission. We further acknowledge your response submitted on
September 29 and October 6, 2023. Acceptability and adequacy of the data analysis will
be a review issue when the full NDA is submitted.

In the final versions of the SAPs submitted on January 5, 2024, the Applicant changed the
handling strategy for ICEs of treatment discontinuation due to an AE to BOCF as the Agency
recommended, while the handling strategy for ICE of treatment discontinuation due to LOE
remained as LOCF.
Assessment
To address the issues outlined above, results from additional sensitivity analyses performed by
the Agency and Applicant are summarized below.
Sensitivity Analysis 1
Table 23 below compares the results from the primary analysis, in which a single imputation
method of LOCF was used to impute data following treatment discontinuation due to lack of
efficacy, and the results from three sensitivity analyses wherein:
1. A single imputation method of BOCF was used for ICE of treatment discontinuation due to
LOE instead (hereafter referred to as Sensitivity Analysis 1-1)
2. An MI method of BOCF was used for ICE of treatment discontinuation due to LOE, in which
the imputed scores outside the [0, 10] range were forced to be 0 and 10, respectively
(hereafter referred to as Sensitivity Analysis 1-2)
3. An MI method of BOCF was used for ICE of treatment discontinuation due to LOE, in which
the imputed scores were generated from a truncated normal distribution with limits of 0 and
10 (hereafter referred to as Sensitivity Analysis 1-3).

Conclusion

sensitivity analyses as compared to that obtained from the primary analysis, the conclusion that
SUZ is superior to placebo in SPID48 remains unchanged.

Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
Appropriate nonclinical studies were submitted to support the safety of SUZ (also referred to as
VX-548 and VRT-1737548); a major human metabolite, VRT-1875420; and impurities in the
drug substance and drug product. The following studies were submitted: primary and secondary
pharmacology, safety pharmacology in the monkey in addition to in vitro human ether-à-go-gorelated gene (hERG) assays; absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion (ADME) and
pharmacokinetic (PK) studies; repeat-dose toxicology studies in the rat and monkey for durations
of 28 days and 13 weeks; a full battery of reproductive toxicology studies in the rat and rabbit;
and a full battery of genetic toxicology studies (Ames, in vitro chromosomal aberration, and in
vivo rat micronucleus assays).
All levels of excipients in the 50-mg tablet drug product formulation are within the levels of
previously approved FDA drug products by the proposed oral route at the maximum daily dose
of 150 mg; therefore, there is no safety concern from the excipients.
General toxicity studies and reproductive toxicity studies performed in rats suggested SUZ may
pose risks with respect to adverse effects on female reproductive tissues, fertility, pregnancy, and
development. Nonclinical findings that may be relevant to human risks or are causes of safety
concerns are highlighted below. However, overall, the nonclinical data support the safety of the
drug product for the proposed acute indication; therefore, the new drug application (NDA) may
be approved from a nonclinical perspective with the recommended labeling changes, which
describe risks for reproductive and developmental effects based on animal studies.

7.1.1. General Toxicology Findings
The systemic safety of SUZ and a major human metabolite, M6-SUZ (also referred to as M6 and
VRT-1875420), were evaluated in 28-day repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and monkeys. In
addition, the safety of SUZ and impurities/degradants in the drug substance and drug product
was evaluated in 91-day repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and monkeys.
In the 28-day rat study, organ weights of the liver and ovaries increased in the high-dose groups.
However, there were no associated histopathological changes in the liver or ovaries. In the
28-day monkey study, increased incidences of soft/watery feces were observed in treated groups.
The mean organ weight of the thyroid/parathyroid was increased in the females without
statistical significance.
In the 91-day rat study, oral administration of SUZ to female rats for 91 days resulted in ovarian
cyst formation, decreased number of corpora lutea, pituitary gland hyperplasia, and mammary
hyperplasia/dilatation. In the 13-week monkey study, reduced weights of testes (-43 to -46%),
epididymis (-14 to -38%), and prostate gland (-47 to -49%) were observed in SUZ treated males
without histopathological correlates. While the testis weights appeared to be within the range of
organ weights of untreated Cynomolgus monkey of similar ages based on published literature
(Haruyama et al. 2012; Amato et al. 2022), it is unclear whether there were testicular functional
46
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
effects. Of note, no significant changes in these organ weights in the 28-day monkey studies
were noted. Therefore, there is minimal concern for the proposed indication.

7.1.2. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
Reproductive and developmental toxicities of SUZ were assessed in male and female fertility and
early embryonic development studies in rats, embryo-fetal development (EFD) studies in
rats/rabbits, and a pre- and postnatal development (PPND) study in rats.
SUZ doses up to 1000 mg/kg did not affect male reproductive performance when administered at
least 28 days before mating and throughout mating. In a female fertility study in rats, increased
preimplantation loss was observed at oral SUZ doses of 15 mg/kg when administered prior to
mating and through gestation day (GD) 7. These effects on fertility in female rats appear to be
reversible as pre-implantation loss was not observed when female rats were mated following
discontinuation of SUZ for 4 weeks.
In the rabbit EFD study, higher postimplantation loss and lower fetal body weights were
observed at 200 mg/kg/day when administered on GDs 7 to 20. In the rat EFD study, higher
47

Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
SUZ has not been approved in any country or region for marketing. Therefore, no postmarketing
experience is available at the time of this review.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The primary sources of safety data are the two controlled phase 3 studies (Studies 104 and 105)
and an open-label, uncontrolled phase 3 study (Study 107). Additional data are available from
two phase 2 studies (Studies 101 and 102). Studies 101 and 104 were conducted in subjects after
bunionectomy, while Studies 102 and 105 were conducted in subjects after abdominoplasty.
Study 107 was an open-label evaluation of SUZ in subjects with acute pain resulting from a
variety of surgical or nonsurgical conditions. In addition, 13 phase 1 studies evaluated SUZ in
healthy subjects and special populations (subjects with hepatic and renal impairment).
The Applicant performed analyses for the individual studies and two pooled populations. These
pooled populations consisted of the pooled phase 3 controlled studies (Studies 104 and 105) and
the pooled phase 2 and 3 controlled studies (Studies 101, 102, 104, and 105). The Applicant
states that the latter pool is a supportive data pool. Data from the phase 1 and open-label phase 3
studies were not pooled given significant differences in their study designs and/or populations.
The review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded
performing a thorough safety review. No major issues were identified with respect to coding and
categorizing adverse events. This review focused primarily on comparisons between the
treatment arms in the phase 3 controlled studies. The major differences between these two
studies were those relating to the acute pain model studied and procedure-specific subject
selection criteria and anesthetic techniques. Pooling of the safety data from these studies is
acceptable considering the otherwise similar study designs and selection criteria. The use of
descriptive analyses of safety was predefined in the protocol for summarizing the safety
outcomes. Separate analyses of the individual phase 3 controlled studies are described in
Section 17.2 of this review.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
The safety database provided by the Applicant is acceptable for a comprehensive safety
assessment of SUZ for the proposed indication, dosage regimen, and duration. In the 18 clinical
studies mentioned in Section 7.4, a total of 1,883 unique subjects received at least one dose of
SUZ. This included 1,266 subjects with acute pain who received the to-be-marketed dose
regimen in the phase 2 and 3 studies, 169 subjects in the acute pain studies who received a lower
dose of SUZ, 393 healthy subjects in the phase 1 studies, and 55 subjects in the phase 1 studies
in special populations. In the controlled phase 3 studies, 874 subjects received at least one dose
of SUZ at the to-be-marketed dose regimen. In the open-label, uncontrolled phase 3 study, 256
subjects received at least one dose of SUZ at the to-be-marketed dose regimen. A summary of
the duration of exposure in the phase 3 studies is shown below in Table 29 and Table 30.

indicated doses. There were no deaths in subjects treated with SUZ. The incidence of serious

7.6.1.2. Deaths, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
There was one death in the placebo group of Study 105 due to an SAE of pulmonary embolism.
There were no deaths in subjects treated with SUZ.
7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled
Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
There was no substantial difference in overall incidence of SAEs experienced by subjects in the
SUZ arm compared to the HB/APAP and placebo arms. The majority of SAEs were experienced
by only one subject in any given treatment arm. There were no substantial differences between
the treatment arms for any specific SAE. No SAEs were considered to be related or possibly
related to the study drug by the study investigators. Most of these SAEs, such as those related to
bleeding, respiratory disorders, and infections, are known risks associated with surgical
procedures and would not be unexpected considering the surgical acute pain models used in
these studies. Additional details regarding these SAEs can be found in Section 17.3.

7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled
Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
Table 34 presents the common AEs by preferred term (PT) and system organ class (SOC) in
Studies 104 and 105 which occurred in at least 0.3% of subjects in any arm. The percentage of
subjects with any AEs was lower in the SUZ treatment arm than in the HB/APAP and placebo
arms. There were no PTs or SOCs for which the incidence was substantially higher in the SUZ
treatment arm than in the HB/APAP or placebo arms.
No AEs occurred more frequently in the SUZ arm compared to the placebo arm by an incidence
of 1.0% or greater. Notable imbalances in AEs between the treatment arms were generally
favorable for the safety profile of SUZ. Notable findings included a lower percentage of subjects
with the AEs of nausea, dizziness, and hypotension in the SUZ treatment arm than in the placebo
arm, as well as a lower percentage of subjects with the AEs of vomiting, nausea, and headache in
the SUZ treatment arm than in the HB/APAP arm.

7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104
and 105
Analyses of laboratory data did not raise major safety concerns. Median changes from baseline
to the end of treatment were similar between the treatment arms for the majority of the laboratory
assessments conducted in these studies.
There were some laboratory parameters with notable imbalances between the SUZ treatment arm
and the HB/APAP and placebo treatment arms. Table 35 and Table 36 demonstrate that a higher
percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm had creatine phosphokinase (CPK) and
estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) values exceeding certain threshold levels compared
to the other treatment arms. Table 37 demonstrates that the mean changes in creatine
phosphokinase and creatinine from baseline were higher and the mean change in eGFR from

7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Pooled
Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
Figure 4 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver injury
(DILI). There were no subjects in any treatment arm in the Cholestasis and Potential Hy’s Law
quadrants. There was a lower percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm (1%) in the
Temple’s Corollary quadrant compared to the HB/APAP (3%) and placebo (2.6%) treatment
arms. These findings do not suggest a significant relationship between SUZ and drug-induced
liver injury.

Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the

A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN after a postbaseline
ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN. Those subjects who meet total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN criteria within 30
days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN criteria are circled in red.
The within 30 days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and Temple’s Corollary cases.
All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin and ULN are plotted.

7.6.1.8. Vital Signs and ECG, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104
and 105
Review of the vital sign data and electrocardiogram (ECG) parameters did not reveal significant
clinically meaningful safety concerns. As depicted in Table 38 and Table 39, during the
treatment period, there were larger decreases from baseline in the mean systolic and diastolic
blood pressures in the SUZ arm compared to the placebo arm. However, the magnitude of this
difference was small and unlikely to be clinically significant.

7.6.1.9. Subgroups, Pooled Analyses, Studies 104 and 105
Table 40 depicts the number of subjects in each specific subgroup among the different treatment
arms. The finding of a lower incidence of AEs in the SUZ arm compared to the HB/APAP and
placebo arms was generally consistent across subgroups. However, there were notable
imbalances in terms of the number of subjects between subgroups, which limited the conclusions
that could be drawn from these data. There were significantly fewer males, subjects ≥65 years of
age, and subjects of races other than Black, African American, or White exposed to SUZ.

7.6.2. Safety Results, Study VX22-548-107 (Study
107)
7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Study 107
There were no deaths. The incidence of SAEs and AEs leading to discontinuation of the study
drug was low. The majority of AEs were mild in severity. The following analyses of the safety
results in Study 107 are limited by the lack of a comparator given the single-arm design of this
study.

7.6.2.2. Deaths, Study 107
There were no deaths.
7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study
107
There were two SAEs. No SAEs were considered to be related to the study drug by the study
investigators. The SAE of cellulitis occurred in a subject who underwent a brachioplasty, and
postoperative infection is a known risk associated with surgical procedures. The SAE of suicidal
ideation occurred in a subject with situational social stressors that appeared unrelated to the study
treatment. Additional details regarding these SAEs can be found in Section 17.3.

7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 107
The incidence of AEs was similar to that seen in the SUZ treatment arm of the pooled, controlled
phase 3 studies. The most common AEs (≥2% incidence) were generally consistent with those
seen in the pooled, controlled phase 3 studies. The one exception is the AE of fall, which was
more common in Study 107 than in the pooled, controlled phase 3 studies. All subjects who
experienced this AE had undergone a lower extremity surgery, and these AEs were all mild in
severity. In these subjects, the study drug dose was not changed in response to the AE.

7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Study 107
Analysis of laboratory findings did not raise clinically meaningful safety concerns. The lack of a
comparator in this single-arm study limits evaluation of the relationship between SUZ and the
observed laboratory findings.
Changes in CPK, creatinine, and eGFR similar to those seen in the pooled, controlled phase 3
studies were observed. These are summarized in Table 45. There was an increase in CPK at
discharge compared to baseline, which resolved by end of treatment. There was an increase in
creatinine and a decrease in eGFR at discharge compared to baseline. These changes from
baseline were larger in magnitude at end of treatment, but resolved by safety follow-up. Overall,
the magnitude of these changes was relatively small and unlikely to be clinically significant.
Because the end of treatment in this study was Day 14, or earlier for subjects discontinuing study
drug before Day 14, it is unknown whether these laboratory changes would have increased in
magnitude to become clinically significant with longer duration of treatment.

7.6.2.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Study 107
Figure 5 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious DILI. There were no
subjects in the Cholestasis and Potential Hy’s Law quadrants. There were five subjects (2%) in
the Temple’s Corollary quadrant. These results do not suggest a significant concern for druginduced liver injury in this study.

A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN after a postbaseline
ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN. Those subjects who meet total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN criteria within 30
days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN criteria are circled in red.
The within 30 days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and temple’s corollary cases.
All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin and ULN are plotted.
In addition to central laboratory data, local laboratory data may be included in the analysis, if applicable.
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; SUZ, suzetrigine 100 mg loading dose followed by
50 mg every 12 hours; ULN, upper limit of normal

7.6.2.8. Vital-Sign and ECG Analyses, Study 107
Review of the vital sign data and ECG parameters did not reveal significant clinically
meaningful safety concerns. As depicted in Table 46, there were mild decreases from baseline in
both the mean systolic and diastolic blood pressures during the treatment period. The magnitude
of this difference was generally small. Vital signs could have been affected by subjects’
qualifying conditions for this study (i.e., being in the postoperative period), and the lack of a
comparator arm limits the interpretability of these results.

7.6.2.9. Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale Analyses,
Study 107
One subject (0.4%) experienced the suicidal ideation event of “wish to be dead” during the
treatment-emergent period. This was considered an SAE. The subject felt that the suicidal
ideation was situational and related to family/relationship issues and limited mobility due to her
postsurgical condition. This SAE was considered by the investigator to be not related to the study
drug. Additional details regarding this SAE are provided in Section 17.3.

7.6.3. Safety Results, Pooled Phase 2 and 3 Studies
The analyses of safety results from the pooled phase 2 and 3 studies in subjects exposed to a
100-mg loading dose of SUZ followed by 50 mg every 12 hours were generally supportive of the
results from the pooled, controlled phase 3 studies. The incidence of SAEs and AEs leading to
discontinuation of the study drug was low overall, and there were no substantial differences
between the treatment arms. The incidence of AEs was lower in the SUZ arm than in both the
HB/APAP and placebo arms. There were no deaths in the phase 2 studies. Adverse events by PT
were generally consistent with those seen in the pooled, controlled phase 3 studies.
