7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Risk of Negative Effects on Renal Function
Issue
Changes in clinical laboratory assessments of renal function, including eGFR and creatinine,
appear to be more common in the SUZ treatment arm than in the HB/APAP and placebo
treatment arms.
Background
The Applicant is evaluating SUZ for the treatment of pain associated with diabetic peripheral
neuropathy (DPN) in addition to the acute pain indication that is the subject of the current NDA
review. They have conducted one phase 2 study in subjects with painful DPN (VX21-548-103),
which evaluated the dose regimens of SUZ 69, 46, or 23 mg daily compared to pregabalin
100 mg three times daily for a 12-week treatment period.
In Study VX21-548-103, the most common AE in subjects who received SUZ was creatinine
clearance (CrCl) decreased. As shown in Table 47 and Table 48, there appears to be a doseresponse relationship, with the highest SUZ dose group having the highest incidence of this AE
and the largest change from baseline in creatinine. Eleven subjects (8.0%) across the SUZ groups
discontinued the study drug due to CrCl <60 mL/min compared to 4 (7.4%) subjects in the
pregabalin group. Subjects who met this CrCl threshold were required to discontinue the study
drug due to pregabalin labeling recommendations for decreased renal function. The Applicant
asserts that the increased incidence of AEs of CrCl decreased and treatment discontinuations due
to this AE in the SUZ 69 mg dose group compared to other groups was an artifact of study
conduct related to baseline imbalances in CrCl.
Any risk of decreased renal function associated with SUZ should be characterized and described
in the labeling to allow healthcare professionals to assess the benefit-risk profile. The review of
this risk issue in the acute pain program focused on changes in clinical laboratory assessments of
renal function, their clinical significance, and related AEs.

Assessment
Clinical laboratory assessments of renal function measured in the phase 3 studies included
creatinine and eGFR. Creatinine clearance was not measured in these studies. Longer-term data
regarding renal function were generally not available since serum chemistry was not routinely
assessed at the safety follow-up visit in the phase 3 studies. In Study 107, this was assessed only
if a clinical finding during the treatment period required follow-up.
As demonstrated in Table 37, which depicts pooled data from Studies 104 and 105, the mean
change from baseline at 48 hours was negative for creatinine and positive for eGFR in all three
treatment arms. Considering that this pattern was observed in the placebo arm, this overall trend
may be related to the peri- and intraoperative administration of intravenous fluids and a
subsequent dilution effect. The magnitude of the decrease in creatinine and increase in eGFR
was similar between the HB/APAP and placebo arms. However, the magnitude of these changes
in the SUZ arm was smaller compared to the other arms.
Study 107 was a single-arm study; therefore, a comparison of changes in renal function
parameters to another treatment was not possible. However, as demonstrated in Table 45, there
was a positive mean change in creatinine from baseline to discharge and end of treatment and a
negative mean change in eGFR from baseline to discharge and end of treatment. There was
essentially no mean change from baseline in these assessments at the safety follow-up visit.
The magnitude of the changes in the renal function laboratory values described above were
overall small and were considered unlikely to be clinically significant based on the data
available. In the SUZ arm of the phase 3 studies, there were no SAEs or AEs leading to study
drug discontinuation that were directly related to renal function. In the phase 3 controlled studies,
the incidence of AEs in the SOC of renal and urinary disorders was lower in the SUZ arm than in
the other arms as demonstrated in Table 49. There is no notable imbalance towards the SUZ arm
for any of the other AEs listed in this table. In Study 107, the incidence of renal AEs was low.

Conclusion
There does not appear to be a clinically meaningful impact on renal function associated with
SUZ in the acute pain setting. The population for the painful DPN indication may be at higher
risk of changes in renal function associated with SUZ because of potential baseline renal
impairment due to diabetes. It is also unknown whether the longer duration of treatment for
chronic pain compared to acute pain is associated with increased risk of renal impairment in the
painful DPN population. These concerns should be further evaluated in the Applicant’s painful
DPN studies with SUZ.

7.7.2. Duration of Use
Issue
The Applicant is proposing treatment with SUZ
However, there are limited data from the acute pain trials to inform duration of use.

(b) (4)

Background
The proposed indication for SUZ is for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults.
Acute pain is typically sudden in onset and time limited. The draft guidance for industry
Development of Non-Opioid Analgesics for Acute Pain (February 2022) defines acute pain as
“lasting up to 30 days, typically in response to some form of tissue injury, such as trauma or
surgery.” It is well known that a subset of subjects with acute pain will go on to develop chronic
(b) (4)
pain.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Hepatic Impairment
Study 007 compared the pharmacokinetics of orally administered SUZ in subjects with mild
(Child-Pugh A) and moderate (Child-Pugh B) hepatic impairment to age-, weight-, and sexmatched healthy subjects.
There was no clinically significant effect of mild hepatic impairment on the pharmacokinetics of
SUZ and M6-SUZ; hence, no dosage adjustment is needed in these patients.
In subjects with moderate hepatic impairment, SUZ and M6-SUZ exposures (area under the
concentration-time curve [AUC]) were 1.50-fold and 1.15-fold higher, respectively, than those in
matched healthy subjects. Therefore, a reduced loading dose of 100 mg, followed by 50 mg q12h
for the next three doses, followed by 50 mg once daily (qd) for all subsequent doses, not to
exceed 14 days is recommended for subjects with moderate hepatic impairment. Modeling and
simulation of the regular dosing regimen (100 mg loading dose, followed by 50 mg q12h) and
the proposed reduced dosing regimen (100 mg loading dose, followed by 50 mg q12h for the
next three doses, followed by 50 mg qd) indicated that exposure during the first 2 to 3 days was
similar between the two dosing regimens. At steady state, subjects with hepatic impairment
exhibited higher accumulation than did healthy subjects under the regular dosing regimen. In
contrast, the reduced dosing regimen resulted in an approximately 20% reduction in PK metrics
at steady state, including a 17% reduction in maximum plasma concentration at steady state
(Cmax,ss), a 22% reduction in trough plasma concentration at steady state (Ctrough,ss), and a 23%
reduction in daily AUCss (refer to pharmacometric analysis in Section 14.5 of this review).
Hence, the review team agrees with the proposed reduced dosing in moderate hepatic
impairment.
SUZ has not been studied in patients with severe hepatic impairment, and therefore should not be
used in these patients.
Renal Impairment
The mass balance study showed that renal excretion of SUZ is minimal. In a dedicated renal
impairment study, the pharmacokinetics of SUZ and M6-SUZ were not altered in subjects with
renal impairment of eGFR 15 to <60 mL/min (estimated by the chronic kidney disease
epidemiology collaboration equation with adjustment for body surface area). Therefore, the
review team agrees that no dosage adjustment is needed in patients with renal impairment of
eGFR >15 mL/min.
76
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
(b) (4)

Based on overall data, the
review team’s recommendation for food effect is as follows: “Avoid consuming food from
2 hours before until 1 hour after the first dose of JOURNAVX to avoid delay in onset of pain
relief. Subsequent doses of JOURNAVX can be taken with or without food.” Taking the first
dose with food may result in a delayed onset of pain relief.
Relative Bioavailability Comparison Between Phase 2 Formulation and To-Be Marketed
Formulation
Study 011 evaluated the relative bioavailability between the to-be-marketed SUZ 50-mg tablet
and the phase 2 formulation, SUZ 10-mg tablet, at a dose of 100 mg in the fasted state.
At the 100-mg dose, the SUZ 50-mg tablets (to-be-marketed formulation) showed
bioequivalence to the 10-mg tablets (phase 2 formulation) in terms of AUC0-∞ while the Cmax was
approximately 15% lower with the lower 90% CI value marginally outside bioequivalence limits.
M6-SUZ showed bioequivalence for both AUC0-∞ and Cmax. The SUZ Tmax was unchanged
between the two formulations (3 hours) while the 50-mg tablets showed delayed Tmax (10 hours)
versus 10-mg tablets (5 hours) for M6-SUZ. Since the dosing regimen of SUZ is a 100-mg
(b) (4)
loading dose followed by 50 mg twice daily
the minor differences in
pharmacokinetics between the to-be-marketed formulation and the phase 2 formulation are
considered not clinically relevant. In addition, the phase 3 studies (104 and 105) were conducted
with the 50-mg to-be-marketed (TBM) tablet, which achieved statistical significance for efficacy
in comparison to placebo for the treatment of acute pain.

8.2.2. Drug Interactions
Effect of Other Drugs on SUZ
Strong CYP3A Inhibitors
SUZ and M6-SUZ are cytochrome P450 (CYP) 3A substrates. The effect of itraconazole, a
strong CYP3A inhibitor, on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ was evaluated in Study 008. The
coadministration of SUZ with itraconazole increased the AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 4.79-fold
(90% CI: 4.28, 5.35) and 4.42-fold (90% CI: 3.64, 5.37), respectively. The upper bound of the
90% CI was greater than 5; therefore, SUZ and M6-SUZ can be considered sensitive substrates
of CYP3A. The coadministration of SUZ with itraconazole resulted in an increase of SUZ Cmax
by 1.47-fold and a 31.7% decrease in the Cmax of M6-SUZ. These results indicated that CYP3A
is the main enzyme involved in metabolism for both SUZ and M6-SUZ.
(b) (4)

The Applicant proposed that SUZ
Since the increase in exposure of SUZ and M6-SUZ is close to 5-fold, where a 100-mg loading
dose of SUZ can behave as a 500-mg single dose for which there is no safety coverage (the
maximum single dose of SUZ evaluated was 395 mg), the review team proposed the
“contraindication” of SUZ with strong CYP3A inhibitors in the label.
Moderate CYP3A Inhibitors

For the effects of moderate CYP3A inhibitors on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ, the Applicant
predicted an exposure increase using physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling.
78
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
The FDA’s PBPK review team conducted its own analysis. Results of the FDA’s PBPK model
indicated that fluconazole (a moderate CYP3A inhibitor) is predicted to increase the geometric
mean (90% CI) AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 3.0-fold (2.9 to 3.1) and 2.6-fold (2.5 to 2.6),
respectively; increase the Cmax of SUZ by 1.20-fold (1.2 to 1.2); and decrease the Cmax M6-SUZ
by 26% (23 to 28).
Based on the increased exposure with moderate CYP3A inhibitors, the Applicant proposed a
reduced SUZ dosing regimen of a 100-mg loading dose, followed by 50 mg q12h for the next
three doses, followed by 50 mg qd for all subsequent doses for up to 14 days. The proposed
adjusted dose regimen is expected to result in a 25% increase in Cmax,ss, 27% increases in both
Ctrough,ss and daily AUCss. These changes in the PK metrics are unlikely to impact efficacy. Based
on the population pharmacokinetic (popPK) analysis, the proposed reduced dosing regimen with
moderate CYP3A inhibitors is considered acceptable. Refer to PBPK and popPK reviews for
additional details (Section 14.5 of the review).
Weak CYP3A Inhibitors
For weak CYP3A inhibitors, PBPK simulation results predicted a SUZ PK exposure ratio of
approximately 1.1 after a single SUZ dose in the presence of cimetidine. Therefore, no dosage
adjustment is needed with weak CYP3A inhibitors.
Strong and Moderate CYP3A Inducers
The effect of a strong CYP3A inducer, rifampin, on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ was evaluated
in Study 013 (Part B), while the effect of a moderate inducer was predicted using PBPK
modeling.
Based on the observed results, rifampin decreased the geometric mean (90% CI) AUC of SUZ
and M6-SUZ by 93% (91 to 94%) and 85% (83 to 86%), respectively, indicating that SUZ is a
sensitive CYP3A substrate. Rifampin decreased the Cmax (90% CI) of SUZ by 80% (75 to 83%)
and increased the Cmax of M6-SUZ by 30% (14 to 46%).
Based on PBPK modeling, efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A inducer) was predicted to decrease the
geometric mean (90% CI) AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ by 63% (61 to 65) and 60% (58 to 62),
respectively. Efavirenz was predicted to decrease the Cmax of SUZ by 29% (27 to 32) and
increase the Cmax of M6-SUZ by 32% (28 to 36). Therefore, the Applicant proposes that SUZ
(b) (4)
Since the
exposure significantly decreased with strong and moderate CYP3A inducers, the review team’s
proposal is to “avoid concomitant use of SUZ with strong and moderate CYP3A inducers.”
Weak CYP3A Inducers
For weak CYP3A inducers, the PBPK simulation results predicted a PK exposure ratio of
approximately 0.8 to 1.0 following a single 100-mg dose. No dosage adjustment is needed with
weak CYP3A inhibitors.
Proton Pump Inhibitors (Omeprazole)
Study 013 (Part A) evaluated the effect of omeprazole on the pharmacokinetics of SUZ and
M6-SUZ in healthy subjects. Relative to SUZ administered alone, the coadministration of SUZ
and omeprazole resulted in a 28% increase in AUC, a 4.5% decrease in Cmax, and no change in
79
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Tmax for SUZ. Mean SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations with and without omeprazole were
similar over the first 6 hours postdose, during which the maximum effect is expected. M6-SUZ
Cmax (7% increase) and AUC (17% increase) were not affected by omeprazole. Since omeprazole
did not affect the oral absorption or decrease the systemic exposure of SUZ, no dosage
adjustment is needed.
Effect of SUZ on Other Drugs
Certain CYP3A Substrates
SUZ is a CYP3A inducer. Study 010 evaluated the effect of SUZ on the pharmacokinetics of a
sensitive CYP3A substrate, midazolam. SUZ decreased the mean (min-max) AUC of midazolam
by 48% (43-53) and Cmax by 37% (27-45). The Applicant has not proposed any labeling
recommendations for CYP3A substrates. Since SUZ decreases the exposure and thereby
compromises the efficacy of certain CYP3A substrates, the review team included following
labeling recommendations for 1) CYP3A substrates for which minimal concentration changes
may lead to loss of efficacy, and 2) sensitive CYP3A substrates:
•

Avoid concomitant use of SUZ with CYP3A substrates where minimal concentration
changes may lead to loss of efficacy unless otherwise recommended in the Prescribing
Information for CYP3A substrates.

•

When using SUZ with sensitive CYP3A substrates, consider increasing the dose of substrate
drugs based on their Prescribing Information if needed or use alternative medications.

P-gp Substrates
SUZ is an inhibitor of P-glycoprotein (P-gp) based on in vitro studies. The effect of repeated
doses of SUZ on the pharmacokinetics of digoxin (a P-gp substrate) was evaluated in Study 010.
SUZ did not affect the pharmacokinetics of digoxin, indicating that SUZ is not an inducer or
inhibitor of P-gp. No dosage adjustment is needed for P-gp substrates when administered with
SUZ.
Hormonal Contraceptives
SUZ induces CYP3A4, which is one of the enzymes involved in the metabolism of oral
hormonal contraceptives. Therefore, the Applicant evaluated the effect of repeated doses of SUZ
on the pharmacokinetics of oral hormonal contraceptives levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol in
Study 010. Levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol are synthetic progestin- and synthetic estrogencontaining contraceptives, respectively. SUZ did not show any significant effect on the systemic
exposure of levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol as measured by AUC and Cmax. Therefore, SUZ
can be used with oral contraceptives containing levonorgestrel and ethinyl estradiol.
In the label, in addition to levonorgestrel, the Applicant included another progestin,
norethindrone, based on the sensitivity of progestins to CYP3A induction (Wiesinger et al.
2020). Based on this literature article, norethindrone has comparable or lower sensitivity than
levonorgestrel to CYP3A induction caused by a strong CYP3A inducer (rifampin). Since there
was no interaction between SUZ and levonorgestrel, no interaction is expected between SUZ and
norethindrone. The Applicant’s proposal to add norethindrone along with levonorgestrel in the

80
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
label is acceptable. The review team included the following labeling recommendations for
hormonal contraceptives:
Avoid concomitant use of JOURNAVX with hormonal contraceptives containing
progestins other than levonorgestrel and norethindrone. If contraception is desired or
needed, alternative contraceptives (such as a combined oral contraceptive containing
ethinyl estradiol as the estrogen and levonorgestrel or norethindrone as the progestin, or
an intrauterine system) or additional nonhormonal contraceptives (such as condoms)
should be used during the use of JOURNAVX and for 28 days after discontinuation of
JOURNAVX.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
No studies in the pediatric population have been submitted in this NDA application. The
Applicant is requesting a deferral for the initiation of pediatric clinical studies in subjects from
birth to <17 years of age until SUZ is determined to have a positive benefit-risk profile in the
adult population for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain.
The Applicant plans to fulfill the Pediatric Research Equity Act requirement through four
randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled clinical studies in pediatric subjects with
moderate-to-severe acute pain as proposed in the agreed initial pediatric study plan from May 3,
2024. Each study will include two parts evaluating the safety and pharmacokinetics (parts A and
B) and efficacy (part B) of SUZ. Part A will be open-label and used to confirm or adjust the
planned doses of SUZ before dosing additional subjects in the same age group in part B.
The studies will be conducted progressively from <17 years of age to birth per the following
sequence:
(b) (4)

1. Study C1: Subjects 12 to <17 years of age with moderate-to-severe acute pain
(b) (4)

2. Study C2: Subjects 6 to <12 years of age with moderate-to-severe acute pain

(b) (4)

(b) (4)

3. Study C3: Subjects 1 to <6 years of age with moderate-to-severe acute pain.
4. Study C4: Subjects from birth to <1 year of age with moderate-to-severe acute pain.
Two pivotal good laboratory practice (GLP) nonclinical juvenile studies are planned to support
pediatric clinical studies:
1. Study N2: Conduct a juvenile animal study in the rodent model to characterize the impact of
SUZ on the developing brain to support clinical studies in pediatric subjects from birth to
<3 years of age.
2. Study N4: Conduct a juvenile animal study in the rodent model to characterize the impact of
SUZ on the developing brain to support clinical studies in pediatric subjects from 3 to
<12 years of age.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Lactation
Lactation data for SUZ are not currently available for humans. Since SUZ use during lactation is
anticipated and the physicochemical properties of SUZ indicate it will likely be transferred into
breast milk, DPMH recommends issuing a PMR for a clinical milk-only lactation study. The data
obtained from a clinical milk-only lactation study would be helpful in determining whether SUZ
is present in human breast milk, and if so, in what amounts. If a clinical milk-only lactation study
demonstrates a clinically important amount of SUZ in breast milk, further study to examine
systemic exposure in the breastfed infant may be needed in the future.
Females and Males of Reproductive Potential
There are no clinical data to inform labeling related to human fertility. Nonclinical data revealed
increased preimplantation loss at oral SUZ doses ≥2.2 times the MRHD when administered prior
to mating and through Gestation Day 7 in female rats. The relevance to humans is unclear, but
based on these findings, SUZ may impair female fertility.
A drug-drug interaction study was performed with an oral combined contraceptive containing
ethinyl estradiol and levonorgestrel, and SUZ did not have a clinically relevant effect on the
exposures of the tested hormones. Norethindrone was not evaluated in the dedicated drug-drug
interaction (DDI) oral contraceptives study but is added in the proposed labeling by the
Applicant based on the literature comparison of CYP3A sensitivity to levonorgestrel. Other
progestins were not studied and thus, a recommendation either to avoid concomitant use of SUZ
with hormonal contraceptives containing other progestins or to use an alternative hormonal or
nonhormonal contraceptive during coadministration with SUZ and for 28 days after the final
dose is warranted in labeling subsection 8.3.

9. Product Quality
Approval
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) review team has assessed NDA 219209 with
respect to chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) and has determined that it meets all
applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports.
Therefore, OPQ recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective. The requested
expiry of 24 months is granted with the storage conditions of controlled room temperature: 68°F
to 77°F (20°C to 25°C), excursions permitted to 59°F to 86°F (15°C to 30°C).

9.1. Device or Combination Product
Considerations
Not applicable.

I. OVERALL ASSESSMENT OF FINDINGS AND RECOMMENDATIONS
Three clinical investigators (CIs) were inspected for each of the two pivotal Studies
VX22-548-104 and VX22-548-105 in support of this original NDA review: (1) Dr.

Jessica McCoun (Atlanta, GA). The data from the inspected CI sites appear to be
acceptable in support of the proposed indication according to the inspection reports.

Financial Disclosure Review
The Applicant adequately disclosed financial interests/arrangements with clinical investigators
per 21 CFR part 54. They provided a completed Form 3454 for Studies 104, 105, and 107. They
certified that they had not entered into any financial arrangements with the listed clinical
investigators as defined in 21 CFR 54.2(a), that no listed clinical investigator who was required
to disclose any financial interests as defined in 21 CFR 54.2(b) disclosed any such interests, and
that no listed investigator was the recipient of significant payments from the Applicant as defined
in 21 CFR 54.2(f).
As noted in the minutes from the December 13, 2023, type B guidance meeting, “Study 107,
while supportive of the positive results of Study 104 and 105, will not be relied upon to establish
the efficacy of SUZ, and therefore, does not require a financial disclosure.” As noted in the
meeting discussion, “although Study 107 is not designed to evaluate a specific safety signal and
may not meet the criteria for a covered clinical study (i.e., studies for which financial disclosure
is required), Vertex may submit a financial disclosure for Study 107 for completeness of the
NDA submission.”
The investigator financial disclosures do not raise questions about the integrity of the data.

11. Advisory Committee Summary
SUZ was not taken to an FDA advisory committee because no unexpected significant safety or
efficacy issues were identified, and no controversial issues arose that would benefit from
advisory committee discussion.

III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
On May 30, 2024, Vertex Pharmaceuticals Inc. (Vertex) submitted an original new molecular
entity new drug application (NDA 219209) for suzetrigine (SUZ, also referred to as VX-548 and
VRT-1737548) tablets, a selective voltage-gated sodium channel (NaV1.8) inhibitor, for the
treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain in adults. NDA 219209 received a priority review
determination and was filed under Section 505(b) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act
(FDCA) on July 30, 2024. The NDA was reviewed according to the provisions of “the Program,”
with a priority designation, under the Prescription Drug User Fee Act VII.
Vertex Pharmaceuticals initiated the SUZ development program under investigational new drug
application (IND) 146185, which was submitted on January 8, 2020. The Applicant conducted
two randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled phase 3 studies, VX22-548-104: A
Phase 3, Randomized, Double‑blind, Placebo‑controlled Study Evaluating the Efficacy and
Safety of Suzetrigine for Acute Pain After a Bunionectomy (Study 104) and VX22-548-105: A
Phase 3, Randomized, Double‑blind, Placebo‑controlled Study Evaluating the Efficacy and
Safety of Suzetrigine for Acute Pain After an Abdominoplasty (Study 105), which were the
primary sources of efficacy data for SUZ. Studies 104 and 105 provided controlled safety data
over a 48-hour treatment period, while VX22-548-107: A Phase 3, Single-arm Study Evaluating
the Safety and Effectiveness of Suzetrigine for Acute Pain (Study 107) provided uncontrolled
safety data over a treatment period of up to 14 days. Study 107 was a phase 3 open-label study
that evaluated subjects with moderate-to-severe acute pain from a variety of surgical and
nonsurgical conditions.
Notable regulatory milestones for this application include:
•

Fast Track designation for SUZ for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute pain was
granted on March 5, 2020.

•

Breakthrough Therapy designation for SUZ for the treatment of moderate-to-severe acute
pain was granted on June 30, 2022.

•

Rolling review was granted on March 18, 2024.

Milestone meetings related to the request for marketing approval:
•

A type B, end-of-phase 2 (EOP2) meeting was held on July 14, 2022, to obtain the Division’s
input on the proposed development plan and phase 3 clinical studies to support the
registration of SUZ for the indication to treat moderate-to-severe acute pain. Vertex’s
planned pivotal phase 3 program consisted of two adequate and well-controlled studies, one
in bunionectomy and the other in abdominoplasty, and a single-arm safety and effectiveness
study in a broad range of surgical and nonsurgical acute pain conditions.

•

Type B, EOP2, chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) only, written responses were
sent to Vertex on December 1, 2022, with preliminary feedback on Vertex’s proposed
regulatory starting materials, impurity control strategy, and SUZ drug substance formal
stability data proposal.
85

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
•

Type B, Breakthrough Therapy, written responses were sent to Vertex on December 22,
2022, regarding Vertex’s abuse liability assessment plan. The Controlled Substance Staff
(CSS) agreed that no further nonclinical studies or human abuse potential studies were
required at that time to assess the abuse potential of SUZ. CSS suggested that Vertex conduct
an animal physical dependence and withdrawal study for assessment of dependence. The
requested physical dependence and withdrawal study was conducted in rats, and the study
design was agreed upon with CSS prior the study initiation. The results and report were
provided at the time of NDA submission.

•

A type D, guidance, CMC-only meeting was held on June 8, 2023, to discuss Vertex’s
intention to leverage FDA Manual of Policies and Procedures 5015.13 to expedite the time to
NDA submission, assessment, and marketing approval for SUZ and to receive Agency
feedback regarding the proposed SUZ drug substance and drug product stability data
package.

•

A type B, guidance meeting was held on December 13, 2023, to obtain alignment with the
Division on several procedural topics related to the upcoming NDA submission and
adequacy for filing for SUZ.

On May 30, 2024, Vertex submitted a proposed proprietary name request for JOURNAVX,
which was found conditionally acceptable on August 15, 2024. The midcycle communication
meeting occurred on October 2, 2024, with minutes issued on November 1, 2024. Labeling
negotiations for the NDA were opened with Vertex on November 26, 2024. The late-cycle
communication meeting occurred on December 5, 2024, with meeting minutes issued on January
2, 2025. Pediatric studies required by section 505B(a) of the FDCA were deferred for birth to
less than 17 years, and four clinical postmarketing requirements (PMRs) and two nonclinical
PMRs were issued. PMRs were also issued under section 505(o)(3) of the FDCA for a pregnancy
exposure registry study, a complementary pregnancy study, and a clinical lactation study.

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With
the Investigational New Drug Application
The nonclinical safety profile of SUZ (also referred to as VRT-1737548 and VX-548) supporting
this NDA was originally reviewed under IND 146185. All nonclinical safety studies conducted
in support of SUZ were also submitted to the present NDA and are reviewed in the following
sections.

13.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
SUZ is a selective human voltage-gated sodium channel subtype 1.8 (NaV1.8) blocker. The
selectivity of SUZ relative to other known voltage-gated sodium channels (NaV 1.1 through 1.9)
was tested in vitro, and the half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) of SUZ on NaV1.8 was
0.7nM, over 30,000× lower than the IC50 of other NaV channels. In addition, SUZ was tested for its
inhibitory activity of NaV1.8 in dorsal root ganglion (DRG) neurons of human, monkey, rat,
86
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
mouse, rabbit, and dog in vitro for species selectivity. SUZ was at least 70-fold less potent in
rodent NaV1.8 than in human or monkey NaV1.8. Of note, a major human metabolite, M6-SUZ
(also referred to as VRT-1875420 and M6), is also a potent human NaV1.8 blocker with an IC50
value of 0.5nM. See Table 53 for additional details.

13.1.2. Secondary Pharmacology
Secondary pharmacology studies of SUZ and M6-SUZ (a major human metabolite) were
conducted to screen activity/binding against other ion channels, receptors, enzyme, and
transporters (176 targets). The in vitro secondary pharmacology screens demonstrated SUZ binds
to several targets, including the human glucocorticoid receptor, human OX1 receptor, human
progesterone receptor (PR)-B (80%), and human norepinephrine transporter, with the highest
binding affinity to the human progesterone receptor (IC50: 0.39µM) out of the targets evaluated.

The Applicant compared IC50 values of SUZ against human versus rat

approximately 94-fold differences.

than the IC50 value of mifepristone to human PR reported in the public
domain and in the assay technical manual.

13.1.3. Safety Pharmacology
SUZ and M6-SUZ were evaluated in good laboratory practice (GLP) in vitro human ether-à-gogo-related gene (hERG) assays, and they had no effect on hERG channel activity (IC50 greater than
10µM). SUZ was tested for effects on the central nervous system (CNS), cardiovascular system,
and respiratory system in a GLP single-dose safety pharmacology study and a GLP 28-day repeatdose toxicity study in monkeys. SUZ and M6-SUZ had no effects on CNS, cardiovascular, and
91
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
respiratory parameters at doses up to 100 mg/kg. Of note, M6-SUZ, which is a major human
metabolite, is also a major metabolite in monkeys. Based on toxicokinetic data of this metabolite in
the 28-day repeat-dose toxicity study in monkeys, there are approximately ≥2× safety margin for
the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of suzetrigine (i.e., 100 mg loading dose and
50 mg maintenance dose every 12 hours).

13.1.4. ADME/PK
The in vitro and in vivo studies designed to assess absorption, distribution, metabolism, and
excretion (ADME) and pharmacokinetics are described below. Based on similarities in potency
against NaV1.8 and metabolic profile between monkeys and humans, monkeys were considered
an appropriate species for use in the pivotal nonclinical safety studies. Additionally, the
Applicant selected the rat as the rodent species based on the metabolism profile and achievable free
drug maximum plasma concentration (Cmax), which was up to 9-fold higher than the rat IC50 for
NaV1.8 inhibition in vitro. While there appeared to be significant potency differences
(approximately 80-folds) for SUZ inhibition of rat versus human NaV1.8 in vitro, the Applicant

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
13.1.4.2. Distribution
Plasma protein binding of SUZ, M6-SUZ, and M3-SUZ, which was evaluated in vitro, was high
and similar across species (i.e., >98% in rat and monkey and ≥99% in dog and human for SUZ;
>91% in rat, monkey, and human and 99% in dog for M6-SUZ; >97% in rat, monkey, dog, and
human for M3-SUZ).
Volume of distribution at steady state of SUZ was 1.01, 3.54, and 2.18 L/kg after an intravenous
dose of 1 mg/kg in male beagle dogs, Sprague-Dawley rats, and cynomolgus monkeys,
respectively.
Distribution to CNS was observed in rats (brain and spinal cord) after oral administration and in
monkeys (cerebrospinal fluid, brain, spinal cord, and DRG) after IV or oral administration. See
Table 66 through Table 70 below.
Radioactivity concentrations in melanin-containing tissues in rats (i.e., the uveal tract, pigmented
skin, and meninges) peaked between 1 and 4 hours postdose then declined to undetectable levels
by 72 hours postdose. Therefore, binding to melanin-containing tissues does not appear to be
irreversible.

Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
13.1.4.3. Metabolism
Figure 8. Proposed Biotransformation Pathway of SUZ

Source: Study P158, Page 16, Figure 4
Abbreviations: SUZ, suzetrigine

SUZ was mainly cleared by metabolism in rats, and unchanged SUZ was not detected in bile and
was minimal in urine (<0.1%) and feces (0.2%) after IV administration. In rats, M3-SUZ was the
major metabolite in plasma (95.9%). M19-SUZ and M20-SUZ were major metabolites in rat bile.
Figure 9. Structural and Description of Moieties of SUZ

Source: Study Q262, Page 9, Figure 1
Abbreviation: SUZ, suzetrigine; THF, tetrahydrofuran

101
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Figure 10. Proposed Biotransformation Pathway of SUZ in Rats

Source: Study Q262, Page 40, Figure 2
Abbreviations: SUZ, suzetrigine (VX-548)

In monkeys, M6-SUZ and M12-SUZ were major metabolites in the plasma. M20-SUZ and M38SUZ were major metabolites in monkey bile. Unchanged SUZ was not detected in bile and was
minimal in feces (<2% of the radioactive dose) and urine (1% of the radioactive dose) following
both oral and IV dosing in monkeys.

102
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Figure 11. Proposed Biotransformation Pathway of SUZ in Monkeys

Source: Study S001, Page 33, Figure 2
Abbreviations: SUZ, suzetrigine (VX-548)

Cytochrome P450 (CYP) 3A4, CYP3A5, and CYP2C19 were major SUZ biotransformation
enzymes in vitro. M6-SUZ was metabolized to M12-SUZ by CYP3A4 and CYP2C9 in vitro.
Non-CYP contribution was 20 to 30% in vitro.

13.1.4.4. Excretion
Biliary Excretion
In rats, 72 to 75% of the administered dose was excreted into bile after oral and IV
administration. In monkeys, 75% of the administered dose was excreted into bile after the IV
administration.
Placental Transfer/Lacteal Excretion
In GD 13 Sprague-Dawley rat fetuses, the highest radioactivity concentrations were observed in
the lungs and spinal cord at 8 hours postdose.
In GD 18 Sprague-Dawley rat fetuses, the highest radioactivity concentrations were observed in
the heart, spinal cord, and adrenal gland.
In lactating Sprague-Dawley rats (lactation day 10), exposure of total radioactivity in milk was
approximately 3-fold higher than in blood and plasma after a single oral administration
(10 mg/kg), with peak concentration at 2 hours postdose.

13.1.5. General Toxicology
Systemic safety of SUZ and M6-SUZ were evaluated in 28-day repeat-dose toxicity studies in
rats and monkeys. In addition, the safety of SUZ and impurities/degradants in the drug
substance/drug product were evaluated in 91-day repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and
monkeys.
In the 28-day rat study, weights of the liver and ovaries increased in the high-dose groups.
However, there were no associated histopathological changes in the liver or ovaries. In the 28day monkey study, increased incidences of soft/watery feces were observed in treated groups.
The mean organ weight of the thyroid/parathyroid was increased in females without statistical
significance. The no-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL) was determined to be
1,000 mg/kg/day (male) and 300 mg/kg/day (female) for rats and 100 mg/kg/day for both sexes
in monkeys.

115
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
In a 91-day rat study, males were dosed at 0, 200, 600, and 1,000 mg/kg/day and females at 0,
10, 100, and 1,000 mg/kg/day via oral gavage. Ploughing, salivation, and wet fur at the mouth
were observed in test article-treated animals at ≥100 mg/kg/day. Minimal-to-moderate ovarian
follicular cysts were observed in female rats treated with the test article at ≥100 mg/kg/day.
Higher incidences of minimal-to-mild decreased number of corpora lutea, minimal-to-mild
distalis hyperplasia in the pituitary gland, moderate-to-marked dilatation in the mammary gland
were observed in females treated at 100 mg/kg compared to the concurrent and historical
controls. Minimal-to-mild cortical hypertrophy in the adrenal gland was observed in females
treated at 1,000 mg/kg/day, which could be a result of stress responses. The NOAELs for males
and females were 1,000 mg/kg/day and 10 mg/kg/day, respectively.
In a 13-week monkey study, animals were treated with SUZ at 0, 20, 50, and 150 mg/kg via oral
gavage. Of note, no recovery group was included in this study. Test article-treated animals
showed increased soft/liquid feces and decreased thymus weight, which is associated with
decreased thymic cellularity, which could be related to stress responses. In addition, reduced
weights of testis, epididymis, and prostate gland were observed in SUZ treated males; however,
there were no adverse microscopic findings while all animals including the control group showed
immaturity in the testis. There were no test article-related changes in body weights,
ophthalmoscopy/electrocardiogram (ECG)/ hematology/clinical chemistry/urinalysis parameters,
and thyroid biomarkers. The NOAEL for both sexes was determined to be 150 mg/kg.

Animals were dose at 0, 20, 50, and 150 mg/kg once daily for 13 weeks
(N=4/sex/group) via oral gavage.
Results:
• No adverse effects were observed on survival, body weights,
ophthalmoscopy, ECG, hematology, clinical chemistry, urinalysis, thyroid
biomarker, or gross pathology.
• Increased incidences of soft/liquid feces were observed in all SUZ-treated
groups.
• Decreased thymus weight (up to 37%) and minimal to mild decreased cortical
cellularity in the thymus were observed in all SUZ-treated groups, which were
considered as stress response.
• Dose-dependent decreases were observed in male reproductive tissue organ
weights across drug treated groups. Epididymis weights were decreased from
−14 to −38% compared to control. Testis weights were decreased from −43
to −46% compared to control. Prostate weights were decreased from −47 to
−49% compared to control. However, there were no microscopic correlates.
In addition, the mean organ weights observed in the SUZ-treated groups
were within the ranges of those in untreated cynomolgus monkeys of similar
age. Also of note, changes in these organ weights in the 28-day and 9-month
monkey studies were generally of lower magnitude, did not follow a dosedependent trend for incidence, and the standard deviations were often very
large and, in some cases, were larger than the mean values. In addition, the
28-day and 9-month monkey studies did not report histological corelates in
these tissues. Therefore, the organ weight changes do not appear to be
toxicologically significant and do not appear to have relevance for the
proposed acute indication. Of note, NaV1.8 is highly expressed in testes
based on information the Applicant provided. Because of the low number of
monkeys in the study, the lack of recovery groups, and known variability in
male reproductive organ weights in this species, the nonhuman primate data
do not provide an ideal model to evaluate the potential risk of SUZ to male
reproductive tissues in these longer duration studies. Should further
evaluation be deemed necessary to support long-term clinical use, studies in
human could be considered.
• The NOAEL was 150 mg/kg.

13.1.6. Genetic Toxicology
SUZ was evaluated for genotoxicity potential in a full battery of genotoxicity studies, including
an in vitro Ames assay, an in vitro micronucleus assay, and in vivo micronucleus assay in rats.
SUZ was not considered mutagenic or clastogenic in these assays.
M6-SUZ (a major metabolite in human and monkeys) was also evaluated for genotoxicity
potential. An Ames study that tested M6-SUZ demonstrated the metabolite was not mutagenic. An
in vitro chromosomal aberration test was not conducted with M6-SUZ. However, the
chromosomal aberration potential of M6-SUZ was assessed through the in vivo micronucleus
assay that tested the parent SUZ, which was negative for genotoxicity. Although M6-SUZ is a
119
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
minor metabolite in rats, the systemic exposure levels of M6-SUZ achieved in rats after SUZ oral
administration in this study exceeded the steady-state levels in humans at the maximum
recommended daily dose. Together with the negative Ames result of M6-SUZ, negative genetic
toxicology battery of SUZ, and in silico quantitative structure-activity relationship (QSAR) data
with M6-SUZ, the weight of evidence supported minimal concern for clastogenicity.

13.1.7. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
Reproductive and developmental toxicities of SUZ were assessed in fertility (male and female)
and early embryonic development studies in rats, embryo-fetal development studies in
rats/rabbits, and a pre- and postnatal development study in rats. SUZ did not affect male
reproductive performance. However, SUZ produced pre- and postimplantation loss, lower fetal
body weight, and lower postnatal survival in rats and postimplantation loss and lower fetal body
weights in rabbits. Additional investigational studies demonstrated that SUZ affects the estrus
cycle in rats and inhibits the rat PR, suggesting that inhibition of the PR may contribute to the
SUZ effects on reproduction. Refer to Sections 7.1 and 13.1.8 for a summary of these studies and
discussion about the adequacy of those data to support the Applicant’s position.
120
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
13.1.7.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
In a male fertility study, male rats were treated with 0, 200, 600, or 1,000 mg/kg of SUZ from at
least 28 days before mating and throughout mating with untreated female rats, and there were no
adverse effects on male reproductive performance or sperm parameters. Thus, the NOAEL for
male reproductive toxicity was 1,000 mg/kg.
In a female fertility study, female rats were treated with 0, 5, 10, or 15 mg/kg of SUZ from a
minimum of 14 days prior to mating, throughout mating with untreated male rats, and continuing
until gestational day (GD) 7. There were higher mean preimplantation losses in the 15 mg/kg
group. Thus, the NOAEL for female fertility toxicity was 10 mg/kg based on increased
preimplantation loss.

13.1.7.2. Embryonic and Fetal Development
In dose range-finding studies for rat embryo-fetal development (EFD), all pregnant female rats
that received ≥25 mg/kg (GDs 6 through 17) had 100% postimplantation loss, which was a basis
for the dose selection for the definitive EFD (dEFD) study in rats. In a rat dEFD study, pregnant
rats were dosed at 0, 5, 10, and 15 mg/kg of SUZ during GDs 6 through 17. The high-dose group
(15 mg/kg) showed higher postimplantation loss and a lower number of live fetuses. However,
there was no SUZ-related effect on malformations or variations in the fetuses. The maternal
NOAEL was 15 mg/kg and the developmental NOAEL was 10 mg/kg based on increased
postimplantation loss and a lower number of live fetuses.
In a rabbit dEFD study, pregnant rabbits received 0, 50, 100, and 200 mg/kg of SUZ during GDs
7 through 20. The high-dose group (200 mg/kg) showed body weight loss and lower food
consumption during dosing period, lower mean gravid uterine weight, lower adjusted body
weight gain, higher postimplantation loss, and lower fetal body weights. There was no SUZ–
related effect on malformations or variations in fetuses. The maternal NOAEL was
100 mg/kg/day based on evidence of maternal toxicity, including body weight loss and lower
food consumption during treatment period and lower adjusted body weight gain. The
developmental NOAEL was 100 mg/kg/day based on postimplantation loss and lower fetal body
weights.

13.1.7.3. Prenatal and Postnatal Development
In a pre- and postnatal development (PPND) study, pregnant rats received 0, 5, 10, and 15 mg/kg
of SUZ once daily via oral gavage from GD 6 to lactation day 20. Two out of 22 F0 females
dosed at 10 mg/kg/day (9% dam) and 12 out of 30 F0 females dosed at 15 mg/kg (40% dam)
showed fully resorbed litters. F0 females dosed at 15 mg/kg also showed lower body weights (36%) during the gestation period (GD 6 to GD 20), lower food consumption during
gestation/lactation period, fewer live newborn pups, and a shorter mean gestation length. F1 pups
from dams dosed at 10 mg/kg and 15 mg/kg showed a lower viability index from birth to
postnatal day 4 (84% and 34% per litter, respectively, versus 98% per litter in the control), lower
birth weights (13 to 33%), and lower mean body weights (8 to 20%) on postnatal day 21. F1
pups from dams dosed at 15 mg/kg also showed late pinna detachment (4.6 days) compared to
the control (3.2 days). There was no effect on eye opening, pupillary reflex, balanopreputial
separation, vaginal opening, auditory startle response, motor activity, and Biel Maze swimming
trials in F1 pups. There was no effect on reproductive parameters (precoital interval, mating
index, fertility index, or pregnancy index) in the F1 generation. The maternal NOAEL was
10 mg/kg based on lower food consumption during the gestation and lactation periods. The
developmental NOAEL was 5 mg/kg based on lower body weight gains during preweaning at
15 mg/kg, total resorptions of litters, shorter gestation length, decreased postnatal survival, and
lower pup body weight at 10 mg/kg and 15 mg/kg.

13.1.8. Special Studies
13.1.8.1. Investigative Studies
To evaluate the reversibility and effects on estrous cycle in female rats, female rats were dosed at
0, 15, or 100 mg/kg of SUZ for 4 weeks and had a no-dosing period of 4 or 6 weeks prior to
mating with untreated males. There was no SUZ-related effect on reproductive performance
(female mating index, female fertility index, female pregnancy index, precoital interval, estrous
cycle length, or number of cycles), ovarian/uterine examination (number of corpora lutea,
number of implantations, preimplantation loss, number of live embryos, number of dead
embryos, postimplantation loss, or number of early resorptions), or macroscopic pathology.
In a 6-week investigational study, female rats received 0, 10, 100, or 300 mg/kg of SUZ for 6
weeks via oral gavage. While there were no test article-related effects on mortality, clinical
signs, body weight, or food consumption, animals dosed at mid dose (MD) and high dose (HD)
showed a significantly longer estrus state (93 to 120% increase in number of days) with an
increase in the number of females in a state of prolonged/persistent estrus (≥7 days) and a
significantly shorter diestrus state (40 to 54% decrease in number of days). In addition, the mean
estradiol concentrations in estrous state in the MD and HD groups were higher than that in the
control group (46 to 58% increase). Microscopically, increased incidences of ovarian cysts,
increased atretic follicles in the ovary, decreased corpora lutea, persistent estrus in the vagina,
and increased secretion in the mammary gland were observed in the MD and HD groups
compared to the control group. Minimal severity of hyperplasia in the mammary gland was
observed in one MD female and adrenal cortical hypertrophy was observed in two HD females.
In an 8-week investigational study, canulated female rats received 0 or 100 mg/kg of SUZ via
oral gavage. Based on estrous cycle analysis, the SUZ-treated group had started a prolonged
estrus state (7 or more consecutive days in estrus) generally within the first 2 to 3 weeks
following initiation of dose administration. Hormone analyses showed that the SUZ-treated
group showed higher mean estradiol, prolactin, and luteinizing hormone concentrations on both
D2 (2nd day of diestrus) and D3 (3rd day of diestrus). Increased incidences of ovarian cysts,
decreased number of corpora lutea, increased atretic follicles in the ovary, persistent estrous in
the vagina, glandular dilatation, and atypical hyperplasia in the mammary gland were also
observed.

Abbreviations: CYP, cytochrome P450; HLM, human liver microsome; fm, fraction metabolized; MATE, multidrug and toxin
extrusion; OATP, organic anion transporting polypeptide; OAT, organic anion transporter; OCT, organic cation transporter; SUZ,
suzetrigine; UGT, uridine 5'-diphospho-glucuronosyltransferase

Absorption
The permeability of SUZ was evaluated using Caco-2-cells, and SUZ showed high permeability
and low efflux. The apparent permeability coefficient (Papp) values were 12.3×10-6 cm/sec for
Papp(a-b) and 11.5×10-6 cm/sec for Papp(b-a), and the efflux (b-a)/(a-b) ratio was 0.94 (Study
FDD-36383).
Distribution
The protein binding of SUZ and its major metabolite M6-SUZ in human plasma are
approximately 99% and 96%, respectively (Study P171). Both human serum albumin and α1acid glycoprotein contribute to SUZ and M6-SUZ binding in human plasma (Study T095). The
plasma protein binding of SUZ and M6-SUZ are concentration-independent, and there was no
interaction with regards to protein binding between SUZ and M6-SUZ; the presence of M6-SUZ
did not impact the protein binding of SUZ, and vice versa (Study T409).
The blood-to-plasma partitioning ratio was 0.64 for SUZ and 0.62 for M6-SUZ (Study P172),
which was consistent with the blood-to-plasma area under the concentration-time curve from
time zero to infinity (AUC0-∞) ratio of 0.562 in the 14C-radiolabeled human ADME study (Study
006), indicating SUZ and M6-SUZ did not preferentially distribute into red blood cells.

133
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Metabolism
Metabolic profiling of SUZ was performed in vitro using hepatocytes from human as well as
mouse, rat, dog, and monkey. The metabolites of SUZ identified in human hepatocyte incubation
were pyridine N-oxide (M6-SUZ) and O-demethylated (M3-SUZ) derivatives, as well as M3
glucuronidase (M4-SUZ). Trace amounts of an O-demethylated, N-oxide derivative (M12-SUZ)
was also detected. All metabolites identified in human hepatocytes were also identified in other
species (Study P158). The further metabolic profiling of M6-SUZ and M3-SUZ using human
hepatocytes identified that M6-SUZ could be further metabolized to M12-SUZ (Study S164).
Based on in vitro studies in recombinant CYP enzymes and human liver microsomes (HLMs)
with CYP-selective chemical inhibitors (Reports P211, P154, R161, R276, and R184), the
N-oxidation of SUZ to M6-SUZ is primarily mediated by CYP3A, with some contribution from
CYP2C19. The O-demethylation of SUZ to M3-SUZ is primary mediated by CYP3A, with
minor contributions from CYP2C9 and CYP2C8. The metabolism of M6-SUZ is also mainly
mediated by CYP3A4, with some contribution from CYP2C9. Flavin-containing
monooxygenases (FMO1 and FMO3) are unlikely to be involved in SUZ oxidation (Report
P154).
Drug-Drug Interactions
Inhibition of CYP450
The CYP inhibition potential of SUZ and its major metabolite M6-SUZ were evaluated in vitro.
The IC50 values for reversible inhibition of a panel of CYP enzymes in HLMs, uncorrected or
corrected for nonspecific binding of SUZ and M6-SUZ, are shown in Table 113 (Studies P164,
U173). SUZ and M6-SUZ are not time-dependent inhibitors of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8,
CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A4 (Studies P164 and S010). At the efficacious dose,
the estimated unbound steady-state plasma Cmax of 0.0132µM for SUZ and 0.124µM for M6SUZ were significantly lower than the IC50 values, regardless of correction for nonspecific
binding in HLMs, indicating low clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of SUZ and M6SUZ via inhibition of CYP enzymes.
As SUZ is an orally administered drug product, with a 100-mg loading dose, the estimated gut
concentration of SUZ is 845µM (100 mg dosed with 250 mL liquid) and the calculated R1, gut
value exceeds the cutoff value of 11, indicating potential inhibition of CYP enzymes by SUZ in
the gut. However, further evaluation using a static mechanistic model predicted the AUC ratio of
midazolam with and without SUZ was less than 1.25, indicating a low risk of DDI via CYP3A4
inhibition in the gut (Study S309).

SUZ, M6-SUZ, and M3-SUZ showed low inhibition potential for uridine 5'-diphosphoglucuronosyltransferase (UGT) 1A1 (Study P161) (Table 114).

Induction of CYP450
SUZ and M6-SUZ induced CYP3A4 mRNA expression and functional activity in all three
hepatocyte lots in a dose-dependent fashion. CYP2B6 mRNA expression and functional activity
in all three hepatocyte lots were also induced by SUZ and M6-SUZ, but to a smaller extent than
that of CYP3A4. Induction of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19 mRNA expression was
observed in at least one lot of hepatocytes. No induction of CYP1A2 by SUZ and M6-SUZ was
observed (Studies P169, S006). Based on the in vitro data, SUZ was predicted to have the
potential to be a weak to moderate CYP3A4 inducer at the efficacious dose, and the induction
potential of M6-SUZ may increase the CYP3A4 induction risk. Therefore, further study of DDI
risk caused by CYP3A4 induction was conducted (refer to Study 010). As the CYP2C enzymes
are induced via activation of the pregnane X receptor, the same as CYP3A4, but are less
sensitive, the CYP2C induction potential of SUZ is expected to be low (Table 115).

Substrate of Transporters
Based on the in vitro studies, SUZ is not a substrate for P-glycoprotein (P-gp), breast cancer
resistance protein (BCRP), organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP) 1B1, or OATP1B3.
M6-SUZ is a P-gp substrate, but not a substrate for BCRP, OATP1B1, or OATP1B3 (Studies
R243, U016, and R100)
Inhibition of Transporters
The inhibition of major transporters involved in drug disposition by SUZ and M6-SUZ was
studied in vitro (Studies P214, R072, R081, P220, P221, S011, S013, S014). The IC50 values are
shown in Table 116. The calculated R values (Table 117), using the estimated unbound steadystate plasma Cmax values of 0.0132μM for SUZ and 0.124μM for M6-SUZ, were less than the
cutoff value of 1.1, except OAT3, which had an R value of 1.1 for M6-SUZ. These results
indicated low systemic DDI potential via inhibition of the transporters. However, due to high
concentration of SUZ in the gut (estimated gut concentration of 845µM), the analysis identified a
potential for P-gp inhibition by SUZ in the gut (R value of 95.4). A dedicated clinical study to
further evaluate the impact of SUZ on P-gp substrate was conducted (refer to Study 010).

14.2. In Vivo Studies
The clinical development program of SUZ consisted of 13 phase 1 clinical studies, two phase 2
dose-ranging studies, two pivotal phase 3 efficacy studies, and one phase 3 safety study. The list
of clinical studies with PK assessments is shown in Table 118 below.

Study 001 and Study 005 Part A (Single Ascending Dose)
Single Ascending Doses
Single ascending doses of SUZ ranging from 7.5 mg to 395 mg (solution formulation for doses
less than 50 mg or suspension formulation for doses 50 mg and greater) under fasted conditions
were evaluated in two studies, Study 001 (7.5 to 65 mg) and Study 005 Part A (90 to 395 mg).
Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were collected up to 216 hours postdose.
After single dose administration:
•

The highest mean SUZ exposures were 31.1 μg·h/mL (AUC0-∞) and 1.11 μg/mL (Cmax), after
the 395-mg dose (Table 120). The mean terminal half-life of SUZ ranged from 35.8 to 59.8
hours across the single-dose cohorts.

•

The highest mean M6-SUZ exposures achieved were 103 μg·h/mL (AUC0-∞) and 1.05 μg/mL
(Cmax), after the 395-mg dose (Table 120). The mean terminal half-life of M6-SUZ ranged
from 44.1 to 76.7 hours across the single-dose cohorts.

•

M6-SUZ exposures (AUC0-∞) were approximately 3-fold higher than SUZ. This metabolite
ratio for AUC0-∞ (M6-SUZ/SUZ) was consistent across all doses studied.

Dose proportionality was assessed statistically using a power model across single oral doses.
After single doses of SUZ up to 395 mg, AUC0-∞ of SUZ and M6-SUZ increased approximately
dose-proportionally up to and including 171 mg and increased less than dose-proportionally at
higher doses. Exposures were therefore approximately dose-proportional through the highest
138

Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
single dose (first dose) in the dosing regimen (100 mg). Cmax of SUZ and M6-SUZ increased less
than dose-proportionally across the range of doses tested.

Study 002 Part A and Study 005 Part B (Multiple Ascending Dose)
Multiple Ascending Doses
Multiple ascending doses of SUZ (solution/suspension formulation) ranging from 5 mg to
178 mg once daily (qd) for 14 days under fasted conditions were evaluated in two studies,
Study 002 part A (5 to 65 mg qd) and Study 005 part B (90 mg and 178 mg qd). A solution
formulation was used for doses less than 50 mg, and a suspension formulation was used for
doses 50 mg and larger. One cohort in Study 005 evaluated a loading dose of 115 mg followed
by 57.5 mg q12h. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were collected on Day 1 (rich
samples, 0 to 24 hours), Day 10 (rich samples, 0 to 24h), Day 14 (rich samples, 0 to 96 hours)
and on Days 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 11 (Study 002 only), 12, and 13 (predose, for trough levels).

139
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
The mean SUZ accumulation ratio was consistent through 65 mg qd (mean range: 1.65 to 1.98).
The mean accumulation ratio decreased at higher doses (mean range: 1.34 to 1.41).
After multiple dose administration (Table 121 and Table 122):
•

The highest mean SUZ exposures achieved were 12.6 μg·h/mL (AUC0-24h on Day 14) and
1.11 μg/mL (Cmax on Day 2), at the 178-mg dose.

•

The highest mean M6-SUZ exposures achieved were 39.3 μg·h/mL (AUC0-24h on Day 14)
and 2.05 μg/mL (Cmax on Day 14), at the 178-mg dose.

•

Across all dose levels, M6-SUZ exposures were approximately 3-fold higher than SUZ
exposures on Day 14, consistent with observations from the single-dose studies. The mean
M6-SUZ AUC accumulation ratio was 2.94 to 3.75, with no trends by dose.

Dose proportionality of SUZ and M6-SUZ Cmax and AUC0-24h on Day 14 after multiple doses
were assessed using a power model across multiple oral doses from 5 to 178 mg. The Cmax for
cohort B2 (115 mg/57.5 mg q12h) was excluded as the dosing interval was different for this
cohort, making the dose-normalized Cmax not comparable. After repeated doses of SUZ up to
178 mg qd, both AUC0-24h and Cmax of SUZ and M6-SUZ on Day 14 increased approximately
dose-proportionally through doses up to 65 mg, and slightly less than dose-proportionally at
higher doses. The less-than-dose-proportional increase in exposure was likely due to
autoinduction at higher doses.

Trough Levels
Figure 15 shows the mean (+standard deviation) plasma trough-concentration-time profiles of
SUZ and M6-SUZ across 14 days of SUZ dosing in Studies 002 part A and 005 part B. At doses
up to 65 mg qd, SUZ and M6-SUZ trough concentrations increased for approximately 10 days,
while at higher doses, the trough concentrations of SUZ and M6-SUZ increased for
approximately 5 days. The appearance of an early plateau (instead of further increases in SUZ
and M6-SUZ concentrations), may be due to an autoinduction mechanism on SUZ and M6-SUZ
clearance. Trough concentrations were higher for the 115 mg/57.5 mg q12h cohort because this
cohort was dosed q12h (57.5 mg q12h) while the other cohorts were dosed once daily (Table
123).

Study 004
Study 004 was a phase 1, open-label, fixed-sequence, DDI study to evaluate the effects of
repeated doses of SUZ on the PK of oral contraceptives, levonorgestrel (LN) and ethinyl
estradiol (EE) in healthy female subjects. Each subject received SUZ tablets (70 mg qd) for
16 days (Dosing Period 2, Day 4 through Day 19). Each subject received two single doses of LN
(150 μg)/EE (30 μg) (Dosing Period 1, Day 1 and Dosing Period 2, Day 17). Serial blood
samples for the PK analysis of LN and EE were collected up to 72 hours postdose. The PK
parameters of LN and EE with and without SUZ are shown in Table 124. Concomitant
administration of SUZ with an oral contraceptive (containing EE and LN) did not affect the
systemic exposure of LN and EE as measured by AUC and Cmax as the 90% CI of all parameters
measured were within 80% to 125% limits. (Table 124).

Study 006 (Mass Balance Study)
Study 006 was a phase 1, open-label, mass balance study to evaluate the absorption, distribution,
metabolism, and excretion of [14C]SUZ in healthy male subjects (N=8). Each subject received a
single oral dose of 80 mg/200 μCi [14C]SUZ capsules. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis
of SUZ, M6-SUZ, M3-SUZ, and M12-SUZ were collected up to 264 hours postdose.
The results show that of the administered dose of [14C]SUZ (80 mg/200 μCi), 94% of the
radioactivity was recovered, out of which 44.0% and 49.9% were recovered in urine and feces,
respectively, primarily as metabolites. The details are provided below.
•

Of the 49.9% of the dose excreted in feces, unchanged SUZ was 9.1%, unchanged M6-SUZ
was <0.9%, and the remainder was other metabolites.

•

Of the 44% of the dose excreted in urine, the majority was metabolites. Unchanged SUZ and
unchanged M6-SUZ in urine were below the limit of quantitation.

•

In plasma, the median time to maximum concentration (Tmax) of unchanged SUZ was 2
hours. The mean half-life of SUZ was 48.1 hours. Total radioactivity (TRA) in plasma had a
median Tmax of 2.75 hours and mean half-life of 57.8 hours (Table 125).

•

The mean plasma apparent clearance and apparent volume of distribution of unchanged SUZ
were 9.08 L/h and 584 L, respectively.

•

In plasma, unchanged SUZ AUC0-∞ was 19.9% of TRA AUC0-∞, indicating that the majority
of circulating exposure was driven by metabolites. M6-SUZ AUC0-∞ was 63.6% of TRA
AUC0-∞. There were no other major circulating metabolites of SUZ.

•

Mean TRA AUC0-∞ blood to plasma ratio was 0.562, indicating no preferential distribution of
SUZ into blood components.

Based on this study, it is noted that SUZ was primarily eliminated by oxidative metabolism in
humans, as the mean unchanged SUZ recovered in urine and feces accounted for less than 10%
of the administered radioactive dose.

Study 008 (DDI - SUZ With Itraconazole)
SUZ and M6-SUZ are CYP3A substrates. Study 008 was a phase 1, open-label, fixed-sequence,
crossover, single-cohort, DDI study to evaluate the effect of itraconazole, a strong CYP3A
inhibitor, on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ in healthy subjects. The study design of Study 008 is
shown in Table 126. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of SUZ and M6-SUZ were
collected up to 216 hours postdose.
Table 126. Study Design, Study 008

Abbreviations: qd, once daily; SUZ, suzetrigine

Coadministration of SUZ with itraconazole increased the AUC0-∞ of SUZ and M6-SUZ by
4.79-fold (90% CI: 4.28, 5.35) and 4.42-fold (90% CI: 3.64, 5.37), respectively. The upper
bound of the 90% CI is greater than 5; therefore, SUZ and M6-SUZ can be considered as
sensitive substrates of CYP3A. The coadministration of SUZ with itraconazole resulted in an
increase of SUZ Cmax by 1.47-fold and a 31.7% decrease in the Cmax of M6-SUZ (Table 127).
These results indicate that CYP3A is the main enzyme involved in metabolism for both SUZ and
M6-SUZ. Based on the significant increase in SUZ and M6-SUZ exposure, concomitant use of
SUZ with strong CYP3A inhibitors is contraindicated.

Study 009 (QT/QTc)
Study 009 was a phase 1, randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled, thorough
QT/QTc (QT corrected for heart rate) study of SUZ in healthy subjects. The subjects were
randomized 2:1:1 to one of three groups (Groups 1, 2A, or 2B) as shown in Figure 16. Subjects
received a total of 11 days of double-blinded study drug treatment, starting with a single dose of
SUZ-matching placebo on Day 1. Group 1 received supratherapeutic doses of SUZ (200 mg qd
for 7 days) on Days 4 through 10. Groups 2A and 2B followed a nested crossover design for
moxifloxacin and placebo, receiving a single 400 mg dose of moxifloxacin on either Day 2
(Group 2A) or Day 11 (Group 2B). All groups received SUZ-matching placebo on Day 3 to
minimize the effects of moxifloxacin carryover in Group 2A. To maintain the blind, groups that
did not receive active SUZ on Days 4 through 10 received SUZ-matching placebo, and groups
that did not receive active moxifloxacin on Days 2 or 11 received moxifloxacin-matching
placebo.
Figure 16. Study Design, Study 009

Source: Adapted from Clinical Study Report VX21-548-009, Figure 2-1
Notes: To maintain the blind, matching placebo was administered, as applicable, so that all subjects received the same number of
tablets or capsules. On Day 1, continuous ECGs were extracted to establish baseline values for cardiodynamic analyses; PK blood
samples were not collected. On other days indicated by brackets, time-matched PK and continuous ECG assessments were both
performed.
Abbreviations: ECG, electrocardiogram; Moxi, moxifloxacin; N, total sample size; PBO, placebo; PK, pharmacokinetic; qd, once
daily

The SUZ median Tmax was 3 to 4 hours, with mean exposures ranging (Cmax to Cmin [minimum
plasma concentration]) from 1.27 to 0.407 μg/mL on Day 6, and from 1.19 to 0.439 μg/mL on
Day 10 after 200-mg qd doses of SUZ on Days 4 through 10. The M6-SUZ median Tmax was
4 hours, with mean exposures ranging (Cmax to Cmin) from 1.71 to 1.15 μg/mL on Day 6, and
from 2.19 to 1.58 μg/mL on Day 10 after 200-mg qd doses of SUZ on Days 4 through 10 (Table
128).
Based on concentration-QTc analysis, an effect on placebo-corrected ΔQTcF (corrected QT
interval, Fridericia's calculation) exceeding 10 msec can be excluded within the full observed
range of SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations up to ~2.16 µg/mL and 3.0 µg /mL, respectively.
Refer to the QT review for concentration-QTc analysis.

Study 010 (DDI - SUZ With Midazolam and Digoxin)
Study 010 was a phase 1, open-label, 2-period, fixed-sequence, crossover, DDI study to evaluate
the effects of SUZ at a dose of 50 mg q12h on the PK of midazolam (CYP3A substrate) and
digoxin (P-gp substrate) in healthy subjects. The treatment period (Days 1 to 24) included
2 dosing periods (Table 129). In both dosing periods, doses were administered following a 2hour fast. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis were collected up to 24 hours postdose for
midazolam and 96 hours postdose for digoxin.

In this study there was a difference in sample number between number of subjects enrolled and
subjects excluded. In the response to an information request sent to the Applicant in this regard,
the Applicant stated that:
During study conduct, the number of subjects to be enrolled was increased to
approximately 35 subjects due to important protocol deviations relating to PK sampling
that led midazolam and/or digoxin PK data from 17 subjects to be unevaluable with PK
sampling taken outside of acceptable window. Because midazolam and digoxin were
administered 1 hour apart to subjects in the study, most subjects had exclusions for both.

Since noncompartmental analysis (NCA) uses the actual time points, this reviewer conducted
additional analysis including all evaluable subjects and sponsor-excluded subjects. The
reviewer’s analysis showed no difference in the outcome in the fold changes compared to the
Applicant’s analysis. Therefore, the Applicant’s analysis is acceptable (Table 130).

146
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Coadministration of midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A substrate) with SUZ after 12 daily doses of
SUZ at 50 mg q12h results in ~48.2% and 36.7% decrease in midazolam AUC0-∞ and Cmax,
respectively, indicating that SUZ is a weak-to-moderate inducer of CYP3A (Table 130). Based
on the results of this evaluation, for sensitive CYP3A substrates, the review team included the
following language in the label:
Avoid concomitant use of JOURNAVX with CYP3A substrates where minimal
concentration changes may lead to loss of efficacy unless otherwise recommended in the
Prescribing Information for CYP3A substrates. When using JOURNAVX with sensitive
CYP3A substrates, consider increasing the dose of substrate drugs based on their
Prescribing Information if needed or use alternative medications.

Digoxin exposures were comparable when it was administered alone and in the presence of SUZ
at 12 daily doses of SUZ at 50 mg q12h, indicating that SUZ is not an inducer or inhibitor of
P-gp.

Study 013 (DDI - SUZ With Omeprazole and Rifampin)
Study 013 was a phase 1, two-part, open-label, fixed-sequence DDI study to evaluate the effects
of omeprazole (part A) and rifampin (part B) on the PK of SUZ (100 mg) in healthy subjects. All
study drugs were administered under fasted conditions. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis
of SUZ and M6-SUZ were collected up to 216 hours postdose.
Omeprazole (40 mg qd for 4 days) did not affect the oral absorption or decrease the systemic
exposure of SUZ. Relative to SUZ (100 mg) administered alone, coadministration of SUZ and
omeprazole resulted in a 28% increase in AUC0-∞, a 4.5% decrease in Cmax, and no change in
Tmax for SUZ. Mean SUZ and M6-SUZ concentrations with and without omeprazole were
similar over the first 6 hours postdose. M6-SUZ PK was not affected by omeprazole and the
exposure change was less than 20% (Table 131).

Based on the observed decrease in exposure of SUZ, avoiding concomitant use of SUZ with
strong and moderate CYP3A inducers is recommended. For the impact of moderate and weak
CYP3A inducers on SUZ, refer to Section 14.7 Q4.

Study 007 (Hepatic Impairment)
Study 007 was a phase 1, open-label, parallel-group study evaluating the PK, safety, and
tolerability of SUZ in subjects with mild (Child-Pugh Class A) or moderate (Child-Pugh Class
B) hepatic impairment (HI), compared to healthy subjects matched by sex, age, and weight. The
148

Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ was not studied in severe hepatic impairment subjects. In all
treatments, subjects were administered SUZ q12h in the fasted state on Days 1 through 14.
Subjects with mild HI and matched healthy subjects received 50 mg SUZ. Subjects with
moderate HI and matched healthy subjects received 20 mg SUZ. Serial blood samples for the PK
analysis of SUZ and M6-SUZ were collected up to 408 hours postdose.
Subjects with mild hepatic impairment had decreased SUZ AUC0-12h (8.6%) and Cmax (10.5%)
and decreased M6-SUZ AUC0-12h (17.3%) and Cmax (17.3%) compared to matched healthy
subjects (Table 133).
Subjects with moderate hepatic impairment had 1.50-fold increased SUZ AUC0-12h and 1.31-fold
increased SUZ Cmax, while M6-SUZ AUC0-12h and Cmax both increased 1.15-fold compared to
matched healthy subjects. SUZ and M6-SUZ had higher accumulation ratios and longer halflives in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment compared to matched healthy subjects and
subjects with mild hepatic impairment (Table 133).
The mean fraction unbound in plasma was consistent across all time points and was similar
between subjects with HI and healthy subjects for SUZ (mean range: 0.896% to 1.07%) and
M6-SUZ (mean range: 3.64% to 4.16%).

Study 014 (Renal Impairment)
Study 014 was a phase 1, open-label study to evaluate the PK and safety of single doses of SUZ
in subjects with severe (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] 15 to 29 mL/min, estimated
by the chronic kidney disease epidemiology collaboration equation with adjustment for body
surface area) or moderate renal impairment (eGFR 30 to 59 mL/min) and in matched healthy
subjects. The severe- and moderate-impairment subjects were matched by sex, age, and body
mass index (BMI) to healthy subjects. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of SUZ and M6SUZ were collected up to 312 hours postdose. Subjects with moderate and severe renal
impairment showed clinically insignificant changes in the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ. The details
are shown below.
Moderate renal impairment versus healthy subjects:
•

Subjects with moderate renal impairment had SUZ AUC0-∞ increased by 21% and Cmax
decreased by 19%, and M6-SUZ AUC0-∞ increased by 3% and Cmax decreased by 24%
compared to matched healthy subjects. The mean fraction unbound in plasma was consistent
across all time points and was similar between subjects with moderate renal impairment and
matched healthy subjects for SUZ (<1%) and M6-SUZ (3.7%) (Table 134).

Severe renal impairment versus healthy subjects:
•

Subjects with severe renal impairment had SUZ AUC0-∞ increased by 17% and Cmax
increased by 12%, and M6-SUZ AUC0-∞ increased by 13% and Cmax increased by 2%
compared to matched healthy subjects. The mean fraction unbound in plasma was consistent
across all time points and was similar between subjects with severe renal impairment and
matched healthy subjects for SUZ (<1%) and M6-SUZ (~3.4%) (Table 135).

Food Effect Studies
The Applicant conducted two food effect studies, Studies 011 and 016. Study 011 evaluated the
effect of a high-fat meal on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ. Study 016 evaluated various meal
types (low-fat, moderate-fat, and high-fat) and timings of meal administration (60 minutes
predose, at 0h, and 60 minutes postdose) on SUZ and M6-SUZ PK.
Study 011 (Relative Bioavailability and 1st Food Effect Study)
Study 011 was a phase 1, open-label, randomized, crossover study to evaluate 1) the relative
bioavailability of the to-be-marketed (TBM) SUZ 50-mg tablet compared to phase 2 formulation,
SUZ 10-mg tablet, at a dose of 100 mg, in the fasted state; and 2) food effect on SUZ 50-mg
tablet at a dose of 100 mg in healthy adult subjects. Serial blood samples for the PK analysis
were collected up to 216 hours postdose.
Relative bioavailability: at the 100-mg dose, the SUZ 50-mg tablets (TBM) showed
bioequivalence to 10-mg tablets (phase 2 formulation) in terms of AUC0-∞ for SUZ, while the
Cmax was ~15% lower with lower 90% CI value marginally outside bioequivalence limits. M6SUZ showed bioequivalence to both AUC0-∞ and Cmax. The SUZ Tmax was unchanged between
the two formulations (3 hours), while 50-mg tablets showed delayed M6-SUZ Tmax (10 hours)
versus 10-mg tablets (5 hours). Since the dosing regimen of JOURNAVX is a 100-mg loading
(b) (4)
dose followed by a 50 mg twice daily
the minor differences in PK
between the TBM formulation and phase 2 formulation are not considered clinically relevant
(Table 136). In addition, the phase 3 studies (104 and 105) were conducted with the TBM 50-mg
tablet, which achieved statistical significance for the primary efficacy endpoint in comparison to
placebo for the treatment of acute pain.

Administration of SUZ with a high-fat meal at the time of dose (30 minutes for meal

from 10 hours to 24 hours for M6-SUZ) compared to fasted conditions (Table 137). Although
Cmax and AUC after a high-fat meal were not affected, in an acute-pain setting, systemic
exposures at early time points following the first dose are important to ensure rapid onset of pain
relief. Hence the impact of food on the PK of SUZ and its metabolite was further analyzed by the
reviewer as a part of this review.

Study 016 (2nd Food Effect Study)
Study 016 was a phase 1, randomized, open-label, crossover study evaluating the effect of food
on the PK of SUZ in healthy adults. It evaluated various meal types (low-fat, moderate-fat, and
152
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
high-fat) and timings of meal administration (90 minutes predose, 30 minutes predose, and 60
minutes postdose) on SUZ and M6-SUZ PK.
The Study was evaluated in the following three parts.
•

Part A: a low-fat and a moderate-fat meal at 0h4 (30 min predose) versus fasted condition

•

Part B: a high-fat meal at 60 minutes predose4 (90 min predose, including 30 minutes for
meal consumption) and 60 min postdose versus fasted condition

•

Part C: a low-fat and moderate-fat meal at 60 minutes predose4 (90 min predose, including 30
minutes for meal consumption) versus fasted condition

The definitions of low-fat, moderate-fat, and high-fat breakfast are as follows:
•

Low-fat breakfast: ~11 to 14 g of fat and 400 to 500 total calories with ~25% of calories
from fat

•

Moderate-fat breakfast: ~20 g of fat and 600 total calories with ~ 30% of calories from fat

•

High-fat breakfast: ~55 to 65 g of fat and 800 to 1000 total calories with ~50% of calories

Serial blood samples for the PK analysis of SUZ and M6-SUZ were collected up to 336 hours
postdose. The various meal types (low-fat, moderate-fat, and high-fat) and timings of meal
administration (60 minutes predose, at 0h, and 60 minutes postdose) did not affect the Cmax or
AUC. However, the median Tmax was delayed, for SUZ or M6-SUZ (Table 138, Table 139, and
Table 140). Since SUZ is being developed for an acute pain indication, the attainment of
sufficient concentrations in the earlier time points is expected for the onset of pain relief. Refer
to the reviewer’s analysis of food effect based on Studies 011 and 016 below.
