First Dose With Regard to Food
To evaluate the effect of administering SUZ after consuming low-, moderate-, and high-fat meals
at 0h (allowing 30 minutes for meal consumption) in comparison to the fasted state, the plasma
concentration-time profiles of SUZ in the initial hours (0 to 6 hours) were compared [Figure 17
Panel A (Study 011), and Panel B (Study 016, Part A)].
There was a considerable decrease in SUZ concentrations at the initial time points after dosing
with moderate-fat meals and high-fat meals (Figure 17). The individual PK profiles after a highfat meal versus fasted condition and moderate-fat (standard-fat) meal versus fasted condition are
shown in Figure 18 and Figure 19, respectively. This decrease in initial concentrations was wellrepresented in individual-level PK data, wherein multiple subjects showed very low
concentrations for the first 2 to 3 hours postdose in the fed state versus fasted state (Figure 18
and Figure 19). Individual profile data showing low early concentrations in the fed state raised
concerns about potential delay in onset of pain relief. The difference in exposure between fed
and fasted states for various meals was further evaluated using the partial area under the
concentration-time curve (pAUC) ratios of low-, moderate-, and high-fat meals versus fasted
conditions. In comparison to the fasted state, taking SUZ with high-fat meals or moderate-fat
meals resulted in considerable decreases in SUZ pAUCs for up to 2 to 3 hours postdose, while
for the low-fat meals, the exposure was low for up to 1 hour postdose (Figure 20). These results
can also be seen when one evaluates differences in Tmax.
For SUZ 100 mg, both high-fat and moderate-fat meals resulted in delays in Tmax of similar
magnitude (Table 137 and Table 138); however, the Applicant only suggested to avoid high-fat
meals for the first dose. The high-fat and moderate-fat meals delayed the median Tmax of SUZ
from 3 hours in the fasted state to 5 hours in the fed state, and M6-SUZ from 8 to 10 hours in the
fasted state to 24 hours in the fed state. The Cmax and AUC of SUZ and M6-SUZ were not
affected by any of the meal conditions (Table 137, Table 138, Table 139, and Table 140).

Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Administration of Subsequent Doses, i.e., 2nd Dose With Regard to Food
The Applicant proposed that after the first dose of 100 mg, the subsequent doses of 50 mg (doses
at 12h, 24h, 36h…) can be taken without regard to meals (the same conditions employed in the
pivotal phase 3 trials that provided evidence of effectiveness). Since the first dose of SUZ has
considerable food effect, the food effect on the second dose, 50 mg at 12h, was evaluated under a
high-fat meal condition in comparison to a fasted condition. Since there are no direct data for this
evaluation, and SUZ follows linear kinetics at a 50-mg dose, nonparametric superposition
simulations were conducted using the Study 011 data. For these simulations, the first dose of
100 mg was administered in a fasting state and the second dose of 50 mg at 12h was
administered in two scenarios: 1) under fasting conditions (reference) and 2) under fed
conditions (test). The SUZ and M6-SUZ PK profiles of these simulations are shown in Figure 21
(reference [red triangles]: 100-mg first dose at 0 hours under fasting plus 50 mg at 12 hours
under fasting; test [blue circles]: 100-mg first dose under fasting at 0 hours plus 50 mg at 12
hours under high-fat meals). From the simulated PK profiles, it can be noted that for the second
dose (50 mg at 12 hours), the PK profiles (12 to 24 hours) are not different when taken with or
without food. In addition, it can be noted that at 12 hours postdose, there are sufficient trough
concentrations from the first dose (100 mg) on top of which the second dose of 50 mg at 12
hours is administered.
Further, the partial areas of the predicted PK profiles were evaluated (Table 141 and Table 142).
There were no differences in predicted SUZ pAUCs, AUC0-12h, or AUC0-24h between test and
reference arms. The SUZ partial areas of the second dose, pAUC12-14h and pAUC12-16h, were 15%
lower under high-fat meals, which is not considered significant. There was no difference in Cmax
values after the second dose between test and reference arms. The median Tmax after the second
dose in the reference arm was 15h, while median Tmax in the test arm was 17h. Similar to SUZ,
there was no difference in partial areas or Cmax between test and reference arms for M6-SUZ.
The median Tmax of M6-SUZ after the second dose in the reference arm was 22h, while median
Tmax in the test arm was 24h (Table 141 and Table 142).

Recommendation for Food Effect
Based on these analyses, the review team does not agree with the Applicant’s proposal of
(b) (4)
of SUZ.
The review team’s labeling recommendation for the first dose of JOURNAVX is, “Avoid
consuming food from 2 hours before until 1 hour after the first dose of JOURNAVX to avoid
delay in onset of pain relief. Subsequent doses of JOURNAVX can be taken with or without
food.” Taking the first dose with food may result in delayed onset of pain relief.

•

Placebo (N= 54)

postdose.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
This section is a review of Applicant’s pharmacometrics analyses including: (1) population
pharmacokinetic (popPK) analysis of SUZ and its major metabolite, M6-SUZ; (2) exposureresponse (E-R) analyses; (3) dose adjustment for moderate hepatic impairment; and (4) dose
adjustment for coadministration of moderate CYP3A inhibitors.

14.5.1. Applicant’s Analyses
PopPK Analyses
Objectives
To characterize the PK disposition of SUZ and M6-SUZ.
Data
The popPK analyses were conducted using plasma concentrations of SUZ and M6-SUZ from
studies 001, 002, 005, 007, 008, 011, 013, 016, 101, 102, 104, and 105. A brief description of
these studies is provided in Table 147.
The PK data consisted of 19,788 evaluable concentrations of SUZ and 19,788 evaluable
concentrations of M6-SUZ from 1,435 subjects, including healthy volunteers, subjects with HI,
and subjects treated for postsurgical pain.
The baseline demographics for the continuous and categorical variables for these subjects are
provided in Table 148.

pharmacokinetic; POP, population

Method
Nonlinear mixed effects modeling was used to characterize the time course of SUZ and M6-SUZ
concentrations using NONMEM® 7.5. All data manipulations, summarizations, and graphical
outputs were performed using R® (version 4.1.1 or higher).
The base population PK model was developed from prior modeling results using a twocompartment model for SUZ and a three-compartment model for M6-SUZ.The prior model was
parameterized for SUZ and M6-SUZ, including clearance (CLP, CLM), volumes of distribution
(V2P, V2M, V3P, V3M, V4M), and intercompartmental clearances (Q3P, Q3M, Q4M).
Absorption was modeled using both zero- and first-order processes, with a lag time (ALAG)
from the depot compartment and relative bioavailability (F1).
In abdominoplasty subjects, reduced F1 and slower absorption were observed following the first
dose; therefore, a separate absorption model was estimated for these subjects. This model
included an estimated time (MTIME) at which the reduced absorption parameters—early
absorption rate (KAD), early absorption duration (D1D), and reduced bioavailability (F1D)—
transitioned to the standard absorption parameters (KA, D1, F1) used for all other subjects.
Dose-dependent absorption was modeled by including a dose-level covariate on KA, D1, and F1.
The base model was chosen after evaluating various compartmental structures with different
parameterizations for absorption (including first- and zero-order, dose-dependent absorption, and
bioavailability) and autoinduction (using linear, maximum effect [Emax], and sigmoidal Emax
models).
Interindividual variability was modeled as multiplicative exponential random effects, and
residual variability was assessed using a combined error model with both additive and
proportional error, including a logarithmic transformation of concentrations due to the large
range of SUZ/M6-SUZ concentrations.

168
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Covariates were assessed for inclusion in the final model using stepwise covariate modeling,
with multiplicative effects on population parameters. Body weight was used to allometrically
scale PK parameters based on graphical assessment of individual PK parameters and body size
(weight and BMI) before stepwise covariate modeling analysis. A stepwise covariate selection
(forward p-value of 0.05 or ΔOFV of 3.84, backward p-value of 0.001 or ΔOFV of 10.83) was
conducted to assess statistical significance, and covariates were selected based on biological
plausibility, diagnostic plots, and statistical tests. The effects of age, baseline BMI, baseline
eGFR, sex, and race on CLP, and sex and race on V2P, V2M, V3P, and V3M were tested. After
covariate inclusion, model refinement was performed based on goodness-of-fit diagnostics,
standard errors of parameter estimates, and clinical relevance of the covariate effects.
Results
The population PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ were best described in a joint model with SUZ and
M6-SUZ concentrations modeled simultaneously (Figure 24). SUZ disposition included zeroorder depot entry, lag time, first-order absorption, distribution, and nonlinear clearance due to
autoinduction. M6-SUZ followed first-order formation from SUZ, distribution, and clearance
with autoinduction. A second M6-SUZ peripheral compartment was excluded for simplicity.
SUZ metabolism to M6-SUZ was fixed at 80%, based on nonclinical data.
Covariates in the model included the effects of body weight on the distribution volumes of SUZ
and M6-SUZ and effects of BMI and sex on SUZ clearance.
The population PK parameter estimates from the final PK models are provided in Table 149. The
PK models adequately characterize the observed data, as shown in Figure 25, Figure 26, Figure
27, and Figure 28.

The Applicant conducted simulations using the final popPK model to predict exposures over the

Exposure-Response Analysis
Objectives
To evaluate the correlation between the PK exposure of SUZ and its active metabolite M6-SUZ
with the efficacy endpoint (time-weighted sum of the pain intensity difference as recorded on the
numerical pain rating scale (NPRS) from 0 to 48 hours [SPID48] response) in subjects
administered SUZ after abdominoplasty or bunionectomy.
Data
PK data (individual concentrations of SUZ and M6-SUZ) and efficacy response (SPID48) from
phase 2 (VX21-548-101 and VX21-548-102) and phase 3 (VX22-548-104 and VX22-548-105)
studies in subjects who underwent bunionectomy or abdominoplasty were used to develop the
E-R models.
A total of 1,725 subjects who underwent abdominoplasty (N=875) or bunionectomy (N=850)
were included in the E-R analysis. Of these 1,725 subjects, 574 received placebo, 33 were
administered a loading dose of 20 mg SUZ followed by 10 mg SUZ every 12 hours, 134 received
a loading dose of 60 mg SUZ followed by 30 mg SUZ every 12 hours, and 984 were dosed with
a loading dose of 100 mg SUZ followed by 50 mg SUZ every 12 hours. The covariate
characteristics of the data are provided in Table 151.

178
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Table 151. Summary Statistics of Continuous and Categorical Covariates for Subjects Undergoing
Abdominoplasty or Bunionectomy

Source: The Applicant’s popPK report (U058), page 49, Table 26.
Abbreviations: BMI, body mass index; N, number of subjects; NPRS, numeric pain rating scale score; SD, standard deviation;
popPK, population pharmacokinetic

Methods
The endpoint evaluated in the E-R analysis was SPID48. The E-R dataset consisted of mean SUZ
and M6-SUZ concentrations, along with SPID48, for 875 subjects who underwent an
abdominoplasty and 850 subjects who underwent a bunionectomy.
Several structural forms of the E-R model were evaluated to characterize the E-R relationship for
SUZ. Linear, power, and sigmoidal E-R model structures were investigated to describe the
relationship between SPID48 response and SUZ/M6-SUZ mean concentrations over 48 hours.
Baseline NPRS, baseline BMI, race, age, sex, and type of surgery (abdominoplasty versus
bunionectomy) were evaluated as covariates.
Results
A power model was identified as the most suitable structural model. Models were also developed
for the abdominoplasty and bunionectomy datasets individually. The results were similar to those
obtained when estimating a single E-R relationship for SUZ using the combined abdominoplasty
and bunionectomy dataset.
Parameter estimates for the final E-R model are presented in Table 152. Baseline NPRS was
identified as the only clinically relevant covariate. Age was also found to be a significant
covariate; however, its effect on efficacy (SPID48) was small and not clinically meaningful
(<20%, Figure 31), with slightly higher SPID48 values in older subjects.
Mean predictions from the E-R models are overlaid on individual observed SPID48 values from
Studies 101, 102, 104, and 105 in Figure 32. The largest SPID48 response was predicted at the

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
exposures resulting from the 100 mg/50 mg q12h dose, with a consistent response predicted
across the entire exposure range at this dose, supporting its selection as the proposed dosing
regimen.

Dose Adjustment for Special Populations
Objective
To evaluate the appropriateness of adjusting the SUZ dosage in subjects with HI, coadministered
a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, or missing doses.
Method
The Applicant simulated SUZ and M6-SUZ exposures using the phase 3 popPK model (Report
U049) based on data from healthy volunteers and phase 2/3 acute pain studies.
To predict exposure ranges in acute pain patients receiving the phase 3 SUZ regimen (100-mg
loading dose followed by 50 mg q12h), the model simulated a typical subject and 1,000 virtual
subjects. Results were compared to simulations in subjects with hepatic impairment and those
coadministered SUZ with a CYP3A inhibitor, using both phase 3 and reduced SUZ dosing
regimens.

Simulation of SUZ PK in Subjects With HI
The Applicant modified the phase 3 popPK model to simulate subjects with HI. A separate
popPK model (Report U036) estimated the relative bioavailability ratio of SUZ and SUZ/M6SUZ clearances in subjects with moderate HI compared to matched healthy subjects (Table 153).
These parameters were then integrated into the phase 3 model to simulate subjects with moderate
HI.

Simulations of SUZ PK in Subjects Coadministered SUZ and a Moderate CYP3A Inhibitor
The Applicant modified the phase 3 popPK model to simulate subjects coadministered SUZ and
a CYP3A inhibitor. As the effect of moderate CYP3A inhibitors on SUZ and M6-SUZ PK was
not clinically studied, a physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) model was developed to
predict this effect. The PBPK model predicted the relative bioavailability ratio of SUZ in
subjects coadministered SUZ and a CYP3A inhibitor to that of subjects receiving SUZ alone
(Report T415), as well as the ratios of SUZ and M6-SUZ clearances in these subjects (Table
154). These parameters were then incorporated into the phase 3 popPK model to simulate
coadministration of SUZ and a CYP3A inhibitor in subjects with acute pain.

Simulation of Missed-Doses Scenarios
The Applicant conducted simulations using the final model to evaluate the impact of missed
doses on SUZ and M6-SUZ exposure in subjects administered the standard SUZ regimen and in
special-population subjects administered a reduced SUZ dose (100-mg loading dose, 50 mg q12h
for the next 3 doses, followed by 50 mg qd for all subsequent doses). Simulations of the
standard-SUZ-regimen scenarios were performed where one or two doses were missed, and a
make-up dose (50 mg or 100 mg) was taken with the next scheduled dose. A 50-mg dose was
given for one missed dose, and a 100-mg dose for more than one missed.
Results
Hepatic Impairment
The Applicant conducted a dedicated study (Study 007) to evaluate the PK and safety of multiple
doses of SUZ in subjects with mild or moderate hepatic impairment, as well as in matched
healthy subjects, refer to Section 14.2. Based on the increased SUZ exposure, a reduced dose is
recommended in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment: 100 mg for the initial (loading)
dose, followed by 50 mg q12h for the next 3 doses, then 50 mg qd for all subsequent doses. This
adjusted regimen will result in SUZ exposure levels similar to those in subjects without hepatic
impairment. However, M6-SUZ exposures are lower in subjects with hepatic impairment (Figure
33 and Figure 34).

Coadministered SUZ and Moderate CYP3A Inhibitor
The effects of moderate CYP3A inhibitors fluconazole, erythromycin, and verapamil on the PK
of SUZ and M6-SUZ were predicted using PBPK modeling (refer to Section 14.7). Based on the
predicted increase in SUZ exposure when coadministered with moderate CYP3A inhibitors, a
reduced SUZ dosing regimen is recommended: 100 mg for the initial(loading) dose followed by
50 mg q12h for the next 3 doses, and then 50 mg qd for all subsequent doses. In subjects
coadministered a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, this adjusted dose will lead to higher SUZ and
M6-SUZ exposures than when no DDI is present, but will not exceed the maximum exposure
levels at the highest clinical doses (Figure 35 and Figure 36).

Missed Dose Simulations
The Applicant conducted simulations for a 50-mg make-up dose following a single missed dose
or a 100-mg make-up dose following two or more missed doses for subjects receiving the
standard regimen. The worst case was simulated, where the make-up dose was administered
together with the next scheduled dose.
As an example of a missed dosing scenario in the subjects on the standard regimen, Figure 37
illustrates the simulated concentration-time profiles where the scheduled dose(s) at 12 hours
(top) or 12 and 24 hours (bottom) were missed, and make-up doses (either 50 mg for one missed
dose or 100 mg for two missed doses) were taken at the same time as the next regularly
scheduled dose.
If a dose is missed, it is recommended to take the missed dose as soon as possible and resume the
original schedule. If two or more doses are missed, it is recommended to take a loading dose of
100 mg and then resume the original schedule. The Applicant’s recommendation for missed dose
management is appropriate; refer to Section 14.5.2.

14.5.2. Reviewer’s Analyses
Objectives
•

Evaluate the popPK model.

•

Assess the effect of intrinsic and extrinsic factors based on popPK analyses.

•

Evaluate the exposure-response relationship.

•

Evaluate the proposed dose for subjects with HI, coadministered with a moderate CYP3A
inhibitor, or missing doses.

Abbreviation: FDA, Food and Drug Administration

Software
PopPK model fitting was performed in NONMEM 7.5 and Pirana 2.9.9. Exploratory E-R
analysis and plotting were performed in R 4.0.0.
Models
The reviewer utilized the Applicant’s final popPK model for SUZ and M6-SUZ. SUZ disposition
was described by a two-compartment model, with zero-order entry into the depot compartment,
followed by first-order absorption from the depot compartment into the central compartment,
first-order distribution to and from a peripheral compartment, and parallel linear clearances
(metabolism and elimination) from the central compartment. The disposition of the major
metabolite (M6-SUZ) was described by a two-compartment model, with formation by
nonreversible, first-order creation from SUZ, first-order distribution to and from the peripheral
compartment, and clearance from its central compartment with nonlinearity due to autoinduction.
Additionally, the reviewer evaluated the E-R relationship and evaluated the appropriateness of
the proposed dose regimen for subjects with moderate hepatic impairment, coadministered with a
moderate CYP3A inhibitor, or missing doses. Typical-subject simulations were performed to
evaluate the impact of those intrinsic or extrinsic factors.
Results
PopPK and E-R Analysis

14.6. Pharmacogenetics

14.7. Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic
Modeling
The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s PBPK analyses to
predict impact of weak or moderate CYP3A inhibitors or inducers on the PK of SUZ and its
primary metabolite, M6-SUZ.
The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report (Reports S313 and T415),
modeling files, and modeling files and Response to FDA’s Information Request (IR) received on
June 28, July 7, July 24, September 10, and October 1, 2024. The reviewers concluded that the
final PBPK model can be used to simulate the DDI effect of moderate CYP3A inhibitors or
inducers on the PK of suzetrigine and M6-SUZ.
The Applicant has developed PBPK models (Reports S313 and T415) for SUZ using Simcyp
version 22 software. The PBPK modeling objectives were to 1) predict the impact of hepatic
impairment on the steady-state exposure parameters (e.g., AUC0-24 and Cmax) of SUZ and M6SUZ; 2) predict the impact of weak or moderate CYP3A inhibitors or inducers on the PK of
suzetrigine and its primary metabolite, M6-SUZ.
The DDI simulation results indicated that:
•

The AUC and Cmax for SUZ increase approximately 3.0-and 1.2-fold, respectively, when
coadministered with a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor (such as fluconazole).

•

SUZ AUC and Cmax increased approximately 1.5-fold and 1.4-fold, respectively, when an
alternative SUZ dosing regimen was implemented to manage the DDI with fluconazole,
compared to the default SUZ dosing.

•

The AUC and Cmax for M6-SUZ increase approximately 2.6-fold and 0.7-fold, respectively,
when coadministered with a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor (such as fluconazole).

•

M6-SUZ AUC and Cmax increased approximately 1.2-fold and 1.1-fold, respectively, when
an alternative SUZ dosing regimen was implemented to manage the DDI with fluconazole,
compared to the default SUZ dosing.

•

The AUC and Cmax for SUZ would decrease approximately by 60% and 30%, respectively,
when coadministered with a moderate CYP3A4 inducer (such as efavirenz).

Q4. Can PBPK analyses predict the effect of a weak or moderate CYP3A modulator on the
PK of SUZ or M6-SUZ following a single SUZ dose?
Yes, the PBPK model adequately predicted the effect of a weak or moderate CYP3A modulator
on the PK of SUZ and M6-SUZ after a single SUZ dose. The Applicant conducted the DDI
simulation for a single 20-mg SUZ dose (not the proposed therapeutic dose) as a CYP3A
substrate administered 2 hours after CYP3A inhibitor dosing in a 23- to 52-year-old population
(not the intended patient population age range). The Applicant responded to the agency that the
SUZ dose, dosing time, and the participants’ age range in the itraconazole DDI study (VX21548-008) were applied to the DDI simulations for SUZ as a CYP3A substrate with weak to
moderate CYP3A inhibitors. The Applicant conducted the DDI simulation for a single 100-mg
SUZ dose as a CYP3A substrate after CYP3A inducer dosing in 23- to 52-year-old population.
Hence, the reviewer performed a DDI simulation for a single 100-mg dose in an 18- to 65-yearold population. The simulation results are summarized in Table 162.
1. After a single 100-mg SUZ dose coadministered with the moderate CYP3A inhibitor
fluconazole (200 mg qd Days 1 through 28), SUZ’s geometric mean AUC and Cmax ratio was
predicted to be 3.0 (90% CI: 2.9, 3.1) and 1.2 (90% CI: 1.2, 1.2), whereas for M6-SUZ, the
predicted AUC and Cmax ratio was 2.6 (90% CI: 2.5, 2.6) and 0.74 (90% CI: 0.72, 0.77).
2. The geometric mean PK exposure ratio was predicted to be ~1.1 after a single 100-mg SUZ
dose in the presence of a weak CYP3A inhibitor, such as cimetidine (400 mg BID).
3. SUZ’s and M6-SUZ’s geometric mean AUC ratios (90% CI) were 0.37 (0.35, 0.39) and 0.40
(0.38, 0.42), and geometric mean Cmax ratio (90% CI) were 0.71 (0.68, 0.73) and 1.32 (1.28,
1.36) following a single 100-mg SUZ dose in the presence of a moderate CYP3A inducer
(e.g., efavirenz 600 mg qd). The Applicant recommend avoiding concomitant use of SUZ
with moderate CYP3A inducers, as coadministration with a moderate CYP3A inducer (e.g.,

205
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; BID, twice daily; CI, confidence interval; Cmax, maximum plasma

Q4. Can PBPK analyses predict the effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor on the PK of
SUZ or M6-SUZ following the modified dosing regimen?
The reviewer conducted DDI simulations to assess the effect of fluconazole on SUZ and
M6-SUZ’s PK following the modified dosing regimen (i.e., 100 mg on Day 1 + 50 mg every 12
hours for three doses, followed by 50 mg qd on Days 4 to 14, in the presence of a moderate
CYP3A inhibitor), and compared the predicted exposure PK parameters with the baseline dosing
regimen (i.e., 100 mg on Day 1 + 50 mg BID on Days 2 to 14). The simulation using the
modified dosing regimen is needed for the following reasons: 1) the appropriate dose metrics at
PK steady state are more relevant than those estimated after a single dose of SUZ in the presence
of a moderate CYP modulator, 2) SUZ is a weak-to-moderate CYP3A inducer and would have
decreased PK exposure after multiple doses. Therefore, a straightforward dose adjustment using
the AUC ratio derived from the single-dose DDI simulation is not suitable. In the virtual DDI
trial setting, 200 mg fluconazole qd was set from Day 1 to Day 28, and the modified SUZ dosing
regimen was set to start on Day 8. The PBPK-predicted effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor on
the PK of SUZ or M6-SUZ following the modified dosing regimen was that the AUC and Cmax
ratio for SUZ or M6-SUZ was ≤1.5 on Day 14 when compared to the baseline dosing regimen
(Figure 50, Table 163).

15. Study/Trial Design
This section describes in detail the designs of the two phase 3 double-blind, placebo-controlled
studies (Studies 104 and 105) that were submitted as evidence of safety and efficacy. In addition,
this section briefly describes Study 107, a phase 3, open-label, longer term safety and efficacy
study.

15.1. Study of Bunionectomy Pain, VX22-548-104
(Study 104)
15.1.1. Study Design, Study 104
Overview and Objective
The primary objective of Study 104 was to evaluate the efficacy of SUZ in treating acute pain
after a bunionectomy.
Study Design
Study 104 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, three-arm, parallel-group study
evaluating the efficacy and safety of SUZ in treating acute pain after a bunionectomy. The study
took place at 21 clinical sites in the United States.
Subjects were randomized 2:2:1 to the SUZ, HB/APAP, or placebo treatment groups,
respectively. The sample size was 1,073 subjects with 216 subjects in the placebo arm, 431
subjects in the HB/APAP arm, and 426 subjects in the SUZ arm (full analysis set).
The treatment arms consisted of placebo, SUZ 100 mg for the first dose followed by 50 mg every
12 hours, and HB/APAP 5/325 mg every 6 hours. All subjects received the same number of
tablets and capsules every 6 hours in a double-dummy design.
The surgery was performed under regional anesthesia (Mayo and popliteal sciatic block) with
intravenous sedation. A continuous popliteal sciatic block infusion was started after surgery and
remained in place until approximately 3 a.m., but no later than 5 a.m., on post-operative Day 1.
Subjects were on a clear liquid diet (≤8 fluid ounces per hour) starting 2 hours before block
removal through 2 hours after the first dose of study drug. After this period, study drug could be
taken with or without food.
The duration of treatment was 48 hours. The safety follow-up visit took place 14±2 days after the
last dose of study drug.
Ibuprofen (400 mg orally every 6 hours as needed) was permitted as a rescue medication starting
any time after the first dose of study drug through 48 hours after the first dose of study drug. All
other medications with analgesic properties, except for those permitted for perioperative pain
management, were prohibited for five half-lives or 2 days (whichever was longer) before
admission through completion of 48-hour assessments (or after early discontinuation, if
applicable).

208
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
In the postoperative period, supplemental analgesic medications were permitted before the time
of popliteal block removal. This included acetaminophen (975 to 1,000 mg every 6 hours as
needed) until 6 hours before removal of the popliteal block for subjects with NPRS score of 4
through 6, intravenous fentanyl citrate (12.5 to 25 μg every 2 hours as needed) until 1.5 hours
before removal of the popliteal block for subjects with NPRS score of 7 or above, and
ropivacaine boluses and/or infusion rate changes as needed prior to removal of the popliteal
block for subjects with NPRS score of ≥4. Boluses were not to be used at the time of block
removal.
Administration of study drug began once the subject’s eligibility had been confirmed by their
request for the first dose of study drug for pain relief and postoperative pain that was reported as
moderate or severe on the verbal rating scale (VRS) and ≥4 on the NPRS within 9 hours after
removal of the popliteal sciatic block. The first dose of study drug was required to be
administered within 25 minutes after randomization.
Other key inclusion criteria included the following: 1) male or female between the ages of 18 and
80 years, inclusive; 2) BMI of 18 to 40 kg/m2, inclusive; 3) had undergone a primary unilateral
bunionectomy with distal first metatarsal osteotomy (i.e., Austin procedure) and internal fixation
under regional anesthesia (Mayo and popliteal sciatic block); 4) lucid and able to follow
commands; 5) all analgesic guidelines were followed during and after the bunionectomy.
The key exclusion criteria included the following:
•

Before surgery:
– Prior history of bunionectomy or other foot surgery on the index foot or bunionectomy on
the opposite foot as part of this study.
– History of any illness or any clinical condition that, in the opinion of the investigator,
might confound the results of the study or pose an additional risk in administering study
drug to the subject.
– Cardiac dysrhythmias requiring antiarrhythmic treatment(s) within the last 2 years;
history or evidence of abnormal study ECGs that, in the opinion of the investigator or
medical monitor, would preclude the subject’s participation in the study; or history of QT
prolongation or standard 12-lead ECG (performed in triplicate) demonstrating median
corrected QT interval, Fridericia's calculation (QTcF) >450 msec at the screening visit or
on Day -1 (preprocedure).
– Presence of an automated implantable cardioverter defibrillator, cardiac
resynchronization therapy device, or pacemaker.
– History of significant hepatic disease.
– Alanine transaminase or aspartate transaminase values >2.5× upper limit of normal.
– History of severe renal impairment defined as aneGFR of <30 mL/min/1.73m2 calculated
using the subject’s measured serum creatinine.
– History of any sensory abnormality that, in the opinion of the investigator, may confound
the ability of the subject to assess postoperative pain.
– Subjects who have a painful physical condition that, in the opinion of the investigator,
may confound the assessments of postoperative pain.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
– Any prior surgery within 1 month before the first study drug dose, unless approved by the
medical monitor.
– American Society of Anesthesiologists physical status classification of ≥3.
– Chronic use of opioids (pure agonists, agonist/antagonists, partial agonists, antagonists)
or nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) with dose escalation within 30 days
before admission or unwillingness or inability to stop analgesics at least five half-lives or
2 days (whichever is longer) before admission.
– Subjects who have started new medications that have not been at a stable dose for at least
14 days prior to the scheduled bunionectomy procedure and before dosing with
investigational product.
– Subjects with sleep apnea and/or on a home positive airway pressure device.
– History of peptic ulcer disease or gastrointestinal bleeding that, in the opinion of the
investigator or medical monitor, would preclude the subject's participation in the study.
– Evidence of misuse, aberrant use, or addiction to alcohol or an illicitly used drug of abuse
in the past 3 years, or a positive test for drugs of abuse.
▪
•

A positive drug screen for a known prescribed concomitant medication that is not
otherwise exclusionary (e.g., benzodiazepines) will not disqualify subjects.

After surgery:
– Subject had a type 3 deformity requiring a base wedge osteotomy, concomitant surgery
such as hammertoe repair, or any surgical complications during the bunionectomy.
– Subject had a medical complication during the bunionectomy that, in the opinion of the
investigator, should preclude randomization.
– Standard 12-lead ECG (performed in triplicate) demonstrating median QTcF >450 msec
at baseline (Day 1 predose).

Study Endpoints
The primary efficacy endpoint was SPID48 compared to placebo.
The key secondary efficacy endpoints included the following:
•

SPID48 compared to HB/APAP

•

Time to ≥2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo

Other secondary efficacy endpoints included the following:
•

Time to ≥1-point reduction in NPRS from baseline compared to placebo

•

Proportion of subjects reporting good or excellent on the Patient Global Assessment (PGA)
at 48 hours compared to placebo

•

Incidence of vomiting or nausea compared to HB/APAP

•

SPID24 compared to placebo

•

Rescue medication use from 0 to 48 hours compared to placebo:
– Time to first use
– Proportion of subjects with use
– Total usage

Other endpoints included the following:
•

SPID36 and SPID12 compared to placebo

•

Proportion of subjects with ≥30%, ≥50%, and ≥70% reduction in NPRS at 48 hours
compared to placebo

•

Proportion of subjects using rescue medication from 0 to 24 hours compared to placebo

•

Time to first use of rescue medication in the first 12 hours compared to placebo

•

Proportion of subjects using rescue medication from 0 to 12 hours and 24 to 48 hours
compared to placebo

•

PK parameter estimates of SUZ and its metabolite, M6-SUZ

The safety endpoints were the incidence and type of adverse events (AEs), changes from
baseline in clinically significant laboratory test results, vital signs, and ECGs.
Pain Intensity Assessment
Pain intensity assessment was obtained by a subject’s report on the NPRS. Before removal of the
popliteal sciatic block, an unscheduled NPRS was completed before administration of
supplemental analgesic medication. After removal of the popliteal sciatic block, NPRS was
completed after VRS only if the subject’s pain was rated moderate or severe on the VRS. During
the treatment period, subjects reported their pain intensity on the NPRS at each scheduled time
point through 48 hours after the first dose of study drug. In addition, pain intensity was recorded
on an unscheduled NPRS immediately before each administration of rescue medication.

15.2. Study of Abdominoplasty Pain, VX22-548105 (Study 105)
15.2.1. Study Design, Study 105
Overview and Objective
The primary objective of Study 105 was to evaluate the efficacy of SUZ in treating acute pain
after an abdominoplasty.
Study Design
Study 105 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, three-arm, parallel-group study
evaluating the efficacy and safety of SUZ in treating acute pain after an abdominoplasty. The
study took place at 12 clinical sites in the United States.
Subjects were randomized 2:2:1 to the SUZ, HB/APAP, or placebo treatment groups,
respectively. The sample size was 1,118 subjects with 223 subjects in the placebo arm, 448
subjects in the HB/APAP arm, and 447 subjects in the SUZ arm (full analysis set).

216
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
The treatment arms consisted of placebo, SUZ 100 mg for the first dose followed by 50 mg every
12 hours, and HB/APAP 5/325 mg every 6 hours. All subjects received the same number of
tablets and capsules every 6 hours in a double-dummy design.
The surgery was performed under intravenous general anesthesia. Subjects abstained from all
food and drink (except ≤8 fluid ounces per hour of water) from the time of surgery completion
through 4 hours after the first dose of study drug. After this period, study drug could be taken
with or without food.
The duration of treatment was 48 hours. The safety follow-up visit took place 14±2 days after the
last dose of study drug.
Ibuprofen (400 mg orally every 6 hours as needed) was permitted as a rescue medication starting
any time after the first dose of study drug through 48 hours after the first dose of study drug. All
other medications with analgesic properties, except for those permitted for perioperative pain

Intravenous fentanyl citrate (12.5 to 25 μg as needed) was permitted as postoperative

enough for randomization but deemed to be in severe pain per clinical judgement and/or was
unable to swallow oral medications. Randomization was not permitted until at least 15 minutes
after the last administration of supplemental analgesic medication.
Administration of study drug began once a subject’s eligibility had been confirmed by their

moderate or severe on the VRS and ≥4 on the NPRS within 4 hours after surgery completion.

abdominoplasty procedure duration ≤3 hours.

compared to placebo

•

PK parameter estimates of SUZ and its metabolite, M6-SUZ

The safety endpoints were the incidence and type of AEs, changes from baseline in clinically
significant laboratory test results, vital signs, and ECGs.
Pain Intensity Assessment
Pain intensity assessment was obtained by a subject’s report on the NPRS. In the postoperative
period before randomization, NPRS was completed after VRS only if the subject’s pain was
rated moderate or severe on the VRS. During the treatment period, subjects reported their pain
intensity on the NPRS at each scheduled time point through 48 hours after the first dose of study
drug. In addition, pain intensity was recorded on an unscheduled NPRS immediately before each
administration of rescue medication.

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)

15.3. Longer-Term Phase 3 Study, VX22-548-107
(Study 107)
Study 107 was a single-arm study evaluating the safety and effectiveness of SUZ in treating
acute pain. The study took place at 13 clinical sites in the United States. The study population
consisted of subjects with a variety of surgical and nonsurgical conditions resulting in pain that
was moderate or severe on a VRS and ≥4 on an NPRS. Surgical subjects had to have had pain
that was expected to last for at least 72 hours and was expected to require no more than a shortterm (i.e., <24 hours) admission, if any. Nonsurgical subjects had to have had pain for a duration
of ≤48 hours. The sample size was 256 subjects (full analysis set).
The treatment consisted of SUZ 100 mg for the first dose then 50 mg every 12 hours. Subjects
continued to receive SUZ for up to 14 days or until their pain resolved, whichever occurred first.
Acetaminophen 650 mg/ibuprofen 400 mg every 6 hours as needed, up to a maximum of
2,600 mg/1,600 mg in any 24-hour period, was permitted as a rescue medication.
Pain was considered to be resolved if a subject took no study drug during a 48-hour period and
took no more than one dose of acetaminophen/ibuprofen during each 24-hour period within the
48-hour period.
The safety assessments included adverse event monitoring, clinical laboratory assessments, vital
sign measurement, standard 12-lead ECGs, physical examinations, and the Columbia-Suicide
Severity Rating Scale. The efficacy assessment was PGA of the study drug.

15.4. Overall Assessment of the Phase 3 Study
Designs
The phase 3 controlled studies (Study 104 and Study 105) were appropriately designed to meet
their stated objectives. As described in the draft guidance for industry Development of NonOpioid Analgesics for Acute Pain (February 2022), the randomized, double-blind, repeat-dose
design of these superiority studies was acceptable for evaluating the safety and efficacy of a
nonopioid analgesic intended for the treatment of acute pain. The primary efficacy endpoint of
sum of the pain intensity difference (SPID) as recorded on the NPRS from 0 to 48 hours
compared to placebo is acceptable. Bunionectomy and abdominoplasty are established models
for bony and soft tissue pain, respectively. The inclusion and exclusion criteria were appropriate
to select a representative patient population with moderate-to-severe acute pain. Appropriate
monitoring for potential safety signals of interest was included in these protocols.
The design of the phase 3 single-arm study (Study 107) was acceptable for providing supportive
data regarding the safety profile of SUZ, especially for longer term use given the treatment
period of up to 14 days. However, the lack of a comparator in this study limits the conclusions
that can be drawn from these data. The usefulness of the efficacy data generated in this study is
limited from a regulatory decision-making standpoint given its single-arm design and the use of
PGA, rather than a measure of pain intensity, as the efficacy assessment.

16.2. Exploratory Result for the Second Key
Secondary Endpoint
Because both studies failed on the first key secondary endpoint, any analysis of the second key
secondary endpoint (i.e., SUZ versus placebo on time to a ≥2-point reduction in NPRS from
baseline) was considered exploratory.
In Study 104, the median time to ≥2-point reduction in NPRS from baseline was 240 minutes
(95% CI: 117, 477) for SUZ, 90 minutes (95% CI: 85, 110) for HB/APAP, and 480 minutes
(95% CI: 476, 716) for placebo. In Study 105, the median time to ≥2-point reduction in NPRS
from baseline was 119 minutes (95% CI: 90, 180) for SUZ, 475 minutes (95% CI: 236, 480) for
HB/APAP, and 480 minutes (95% CI: 477, 705) for placebo.

17. Clinical Safety
17.1. Consultative Review, Interdisciplinary
Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies
The Applicant conducted a thorough QT study (Study VX21-548-009). Based on this study, SUZ
is not associated with significant QTc prolongation up to two times exposure of the maximum
recommended dose. The interdisciplinary review team for Cardiac Safety Studies Consultative
Review dated July 31, 2024, including recommended revisions to labeling, is excerpted below:
1 SUMMARY
VX-548 did not prolong the QTcF interval in this thorough QT (TQT) assessment study
as described in the ICH E14 guidance – see Table 1 for overall results.
The clinical study VX21-548-009 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo- and activecontrolled parallel with nested crossover, multiple-dose ECG study evaluating the effect
of VX-548 on the QT/QTc interval in healthy subjects. The highest dose studied was
200 mg QD for 7 days (from Day 4 to Day 10), which covers the maximum anticipated
high exposure (Cmax) scenario when VX-548 is administered to subjects with severe
hepatic impairment by 1.2-fold for VX-548 and 0.9-fold for M6-548.
Data were analyzed using exposure-response analysis as primary analysis, which do not
suggest that VX-548 is associated with significant QTc prolongation (refer to section
4.5). The findings of the primary analysis are further supported by the lack of QTc
prolongation in by-time analysis (refer to section 4.3) and categorical analysis (section
4.4). Assay sensitivity was established based on the concentration-QTc analysis of
400 mg oral moxifloxacin.

17.2.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Study 104
Analyses of laboratory data did not raise significant clinically meaningful safety concerns.
Median changes from baseline to the end of treatment were similar between the treatment arms
for the majority of the laboratory assessments conducted.
There were some laboratory parameters with notable imbalances between the SUZ treatment arm
and the HB/APAP and placebo treatment arms. Table 176 and Table 177 demonstrate that a
higher percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm had creatine phosphokinase (CPK) and
eGFR values exceeding certain threshold levels than did subjects in the other treatment arms.
Table 178 demonstrates that the mean changes in CPK and creatinine from baseline were higher
and the mean change in eGFR from baseline was lower in the SUZ treatment arm at 48 hours.
However, the magnitude of these changes was relatively small and unlikely to be clinically
significant.

17.2.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Study
104
Figure 54 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver injury
(DILI). There were no subjects in any treatment arm in the Cholestasis and Potential Hy’s Law
quadrants. There was a lower percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm (0.2%) in the
Temple’s Corollary quadrant than in the HB/APAP (1.9%) and placebo (1.9%) treatment arms.
These findings do not suggest a significant relationship between SUZ and drug-induced liver
injury.
Figure 54. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Study 104

Source: adlb.xpt; Software: R
Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the
postbaseline period.
A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2× ULN after a postbaseline
ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN. Those subjects who meet total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2× ULN criteria within 30
days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN criteria are circled in red.
The within 30 days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and temple’s corollary cases.
All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin and ULN are plotted.
In addition to central laboratory data, local laboratory data may be included in the analysis, if applicable.
For number of subjects in each quadrant, see the table “Subjects in Each Quadrant for Potential Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver
Injury Screening Plot …” and the listing “Listing of Subjects in Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening….”
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; HB/APAP, hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen
5/325 mg every 6 hours; SUZ, suzetrigine 100 mg loading dose followed by 50 mg every 12 hours; ULN, upper limit of normal

17.2.1.8. Vital-Sign and ECG Analyses, Study 104
Review of the vital-sign data and ECG parameters did not reveal significant clinically
meaningful safety concerns associated with SUZ. As depicted in Table 179 and Table 180,
during the treatment period, there were larger decreases from baseline in the mean systolic and
diastolic blood pressures in the SUZ arm than in the placebo arm. However, the magnitude of
this difference was small and unlikely to be clinically significant.

17.2.1.9. Subgroup Analyses, Study 104
Table 181 depicts the number of subjects in each specific subgroup among the different
treatment arms. The finding of a lower incidence of AEs in the SUZ arm than in the HB/APAP
and placebo arms was generally consistent across subgroups. However, there were notable
imbalances in terms of the number of subjects between subgroup categories, which limited the

conclusions that can be drawn from these data. There were significantly fewer males, subjects

SUZ.

17.2.2. Safety Results, Study VX22-548-105 (Study
105)
17.2.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Study 105
There were no deaths in subjects treated with SUZ. The incidence of SAEs and AEs leading to
discontinuation of the study drug was low overall, and there were no substantial differences
between the treatment arms. The incidence of any AE was lower in the SUZ arm than in both the
HB/APAP and placebo arms.

17.2.2.2. Deaths, Study 105
There was one death in the placebo group due to an SAE of pulmonary embolism. There were no
deaths in subjects treated with SUZ.
17.2.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events,
Study 105
There was no substantial difference in the overall incidence of SAEs experienced by subjects in
the SUZ arm compared to the HB/APAP and placebo arms. The majority of SAEs were
experienced by only one subject in any given treatment arm. There were no substantial
differences between the treatment arms for any specific SAE. No SAEs were considered related
or possibly related to the study drug by the study investigators. Most of these SAEs were known
risks associated with surgical procedures and were not unexpected considering the surgical acute
pain model used.

17.2.2.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment
Discontinuation, Study 105
There was no substantial difference in the incidence of any AE leading to discontinuation in the
SUZ arm compared to the HB/APAP and placebo arms. The majority of AEs leading to
discontinuation were experienced by only one subject in any given treatment arm.
There were no substantial differences between the treatment arms for any specific AE leading to
discontinuation. Similar to the SAEs described in Section 17.2.2.3, many of the AEs leading to
discontinuation were known to be associated with surgical procedures and were not unexpected
considering the surgical acute pain model used.

17.2.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 105
Table 185 presents the common AEs by PT and SOC in Study 105 that occurred in at least 0.3%

in the SUZ group than in the placebo group by ≥1.0%. Notable imbalances in AEs between the
treatment arms were generally favorable for the safety profile of SUZ.

17.2.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Study 105
Analyses of laboratory data did not raise significant clinically meaningful safety concerns.
Median changes from baseline to the end of treatment were similar between the treatment arms
for the majority of the laboratory assessments conducted.
There were some laboratory parameters with notable imbalances between the SUZ treatment arm
and the HB/APAP and placebo treatment arms. Table 186 demonstrates that a higher percentage
of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm had CPK values exceeding certain threshold levels than did
subjects in the other treatment arms. Table 187 demonstrates that the mean changes in CPK and
creatinine from baseline were higher and the mean change in eGFR from baseline was lower in
the SUZ treatment arm at 48 hours. However, the magnitude of these changes was relatively
small and unlikely to be clinically significant.

17.2.2.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Study
105
Figure 55 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious DILI. There were no
subjects in any treatment arm in the Cholestasis and Potential Hy’s Law quadrants. There was a
lower percentage of subjects in the SUZ treatment arm (1.8%) in the Temple’s Corollary
quadrant than in the HB/APAP (4.1%) and placebo (3.2%) treatment arms. These findings do not
suggest a significant relationship between SUZ and drug-induced liver injury.

245
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
Figure 55. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Study 105

Source: adlb.xpt; Software: R
Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the
postbaseline period.
A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2× ULN after a postbaseline
ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN. Those subjects who meet total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2× ULN criteria within 30
days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN criteria are circled in red.
The within 30 days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and temple’s corollary cases.
All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin and ULN are plotted.
In addition to central laboratory data, local laboratory data may be included in the analysis, if applicable.
For number of subjects in each quadrant, see the table “Subjects in Each Quadrant for Potential Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver
Injury Screening Plot …” and the listing “Listing of Subjects in Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening….”
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; HB/APAP, hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen
5/325 mg every 6 hours; SUZ, suzetrigine 100 mg loading dose followed by 50 mg every 12 hours; ULN, upper limit of normal

17.2.2.8. Vital-Sign and ECG Analyses, Study 105
Review of the vital sign data and ECG parameters did not reveal significant clinically
meaningful safety concerns associated with SUZ. As depicted in Table 188 and Table 189,
during the treatment period, there were larger decreases from baseline in the mean systolic and
diastolic blood pressures in the SUZ arm than in the placebo arm. However, the magnitude of
this difference was small and unlikely to be clinically significant.

17.2.2.9. Subgroup Analyses, Study 105
Table 190 depicts the number of subjects in each specific subgroup among the different
treatment arms. The finding of a lower incidence of AEs in the SUZ arm than in the HB/APAP
and placebo arms was generally consistent across subgroups. However, there were notable
imbalances in terms of the number of subjects between subgroup categories, which limited the
conclusions that could be drawn from these data. There were significantly fewer males, subjects
≥65 years of age, and subjects of races other than Black, African American, or White exposed to
SUZ.

17.3. Major Safety Findings, Phase 2 and 3
Studies
Summaries of narratives for the major safety findings in subjects treated with SUZ in the phase 2
and 3 clinical studies are provided below.
Phase 2 Studies
Study VX21-548-101
There were no deaths, no SAEs, and no AEs leading to study drug interruption or
discontinuation.
Study VX21-548-102
There were no deaths. One subject in the SUZ 60 mg/30 mg q12h group experienced an SAE.
Two subjects in the SUZ 60 mg/30 mg q12h group experienced an AE leading to treatment
discontinuation. There were no AEs leading to treatment interruption.
(b) (6)

1. Subject
(SAE - pulmonary embolism): This was a 53-year-old female status
post (s/p) abdominoplasty surgery. The subject was randomized to SUZ 60 mg/30 mg q12h.
On Study Day 1, before the first dose of study drug, the subject had an AE of mild hypoxia.
Treatment included oxygen and this AE was considered resolved the next day. The subject
completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day 10, the subject had worsening
tachycardia, hypertension, and hypoxia. The subject was subsequently hospitalized for an
248
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
SAE of pulmonary embolism. Treatment included apixaban and oxygen therapy. The subject
was discharged from the hospital on Study Day 14. The SAE of pulmonary embolism was
considered resolved approximately 10 weeks after the start of this SAE.
(b) (6)

2. Subject
(AE that led to study drug discontinuation - chest discomfort): This was
a 43-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject was randomized to SUZ
60 mg/30 mg q12h. On Study Day 2, the subject had an AE of moderate chest discomfort
(pressure in esophageal area of upper chest). The subject did not receive treatment for this.
There were no ECG or oxygen saturation abnormalities at scheduled time points before and
after the start of this AE. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to this event. The subject
was lost to follow-up and was considered to have discontinued the study on Study Day 3.
(b) (6)

3. Subject
(AE that led to study drug discontinuation – anemia): This was a 42year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. On Study Day 1, before the first dose of study
drug, the subject had AEs of moderate hypoxia and mild hypothermia, which were
considered resolved that same day. The subject was randomized to SUZ 60 mg/30 mg q12h.
Later on Study Day 1, the subject had AEs of moderate anemia and moderate hypotension.
The subject also had high output from their surgical drains. The subject was treated with IV
fluid. Relevant concomitant medication before or at onset of the AE of anemia included
heparin. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to this AE. On Study Day 2, the subject
experienced decreasing hemoglobin/hematocrit laboratory values and hypotension. The
subject returned to the operating room for wound exploration, and a venous bleed was
identified and treated. The subject had an AE of moderate hypoxia, which was treated with
oxygen and considered resolved the next day. The AE of anemia was considered resolved
approximately 2.5 weeks after the start of this AE.
Phase 3 Studies
Study VX22-548-104
There were no AEs leading to death, no SAEs, and no AEs that led to treatment discontinuation
or interruption in subjects treated with SUZ.
Study VX22-548-105
There were no AEs leading to death in the SUZ group. Eleven (2.5%) subjects in the SUZ group
experienced an SAE. Five (1.1%) subjects in the SUZ group experienced an AE leading to
treatment discontinuation. There were no subjects in the SUZ group who experienced an AE
leading to treatment interruption.
(b) (6)

1. Subject
(SAE - hypoxia): This was a 61-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty
surgery who was found to have an oxygen saturation (SpO2) of 89% approximately 2 hours
after the first dose of study drug. The subject’s predose SpO2 was 97%. No symptoms, such
as dyspnea and shortness of breath, were reported but their room air SpO2 remained low
despite regular incentive spirometry, ambulation, deep breaths, and cough. Treatment
included supplemental oxygen. The subject also had moderate AEs of anemia postoperative
and coagulopathy and a mild AE of dizziness on Study Day 3. The subject completed
treatment with the study drug. On Study Day 5, the SAE of hypoxia was considered resolved
with a SpO2 of 95%.
249
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
(b) (6)

2. Subject
(SAE - anemia): This was a 31-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty
surgery who was found to have anemia with hemoglobin of 6.4 g/dL on Study Day 3.
Screening laboratory results showed a hemoglobin of 12.2 g/dL. The subject completed
treatment with the study drug. Approximately 2.5 weeks after the last dose of study drug, the
subject was hospitalized due to the SAE of anemia. Laboratory test results included
hemoglobin of 6.3 g/dL. The subject was transfused with a total of two units of packed red
blood cells and underwent debridement, which showed infection and a hematoma. Five days
after this surgery, the subject’s hemoglobin improved to 8.9 g/dL and they were discharged
from the hospital.
(b) (6)

3. Subject
(SAE - impaired healing): This was a 35-year-old female s/p
abdominoplasty surgery. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day
15, the subject had an SAE of impaired healing. They reported drainage and described
superficial necrosis of the skin at the incision edge, which was confirmed as a wound
infection. The subject subsequently underwent debridement, wound packing, and wound
care. The SAE of impaired healing was considered resolved approximately 20 weeks after
the start of this SAE.
(b) (6)

4. Subject
(SAE - pulmonary embolism): This was a 36-year-old female s/p
abdominoplasty surgery. On Study Day 1, the subject had a moderate AE of syncope, which
resolved after treatment with IV fluids. The subject completed treatment with the study drug.
On Study Day 9, the subject had an SAE of pulmonary embolism and was discharged home
with apixaban for treatment. On Study Day 10, the subject was hospitalized due to an SAE of
pulmonary embolism and presented with right leg pain. Additional treatment included IV
heparin and normal saline. The subject’s breathing improved, the right lower chest pain was
controlled, and the subject was discharged from the hospital on Study Day 12.
(b) (6)

5. Subject
(SAE - pulmonary embolism): This was a 36-year-old female s/p
abdominoplasty surgery. The subject received her last dose of study drug on Study Day 2
(discontinued due to lack of efficacy). On Study Day 4, the subject was hospitalized due to
an SAE of pulmonary embolism after a syncopal episode. The subject also had an AE of
moderate ECG ST-T segment abnormal. Computed tomography (CT) angiography showed
an acute submassive pulmonary embolism. The subject was treated with alteplase. They
started bleeding from lower abdominal drains and went into respiratory failure and shock.
The subject had six vessels embolized by interventional radiology to control bleeding. The
subject was asymptomatic and was discharged from the hospital on Study Day 13. The SAE
of pulmonary embolism was considered resolved approximately 23 weeks after the start of
this SAE.
(b) (6)

6. Subject
(SAE, AE that led to study drug discontinuation - post procedural
hematoma): This was a 29-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. On Study Day 1, the
subject had AEs of moderate nausea and mild dizziness and was hospitalized for an SAE of
postprocedural hematoma. The subject underwent incision and drainage of hematoma. Study
drug dosing was withdrawn due to this SAE. On the same day, the SAE of postprocedural
hematoma and AEs of nausea and dizziness were considered resolved.
(b) (6)

7. Subject
(SAE, SAE that led to study drug discontinuation - postprocedural
hematoma): This was a 34-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject had
increased drainage and slightly increased swelling at surgical site along with tachycardia and
250
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5521299

NDA 219209
Journavx (Suzetrigine; VX-548)
hemoglobin of 9.4 g/dL. The subject was given a red blood cell transfusion and taken back to
the operating room for an abdominal flap exploration, hemostasis, washout, and removal of
700 mL of blood clot. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to the SAE of postprocedural
hematoma. On the same day, the SAE of postprocedural hematoma was considered resolved.
(b) (6)

8. Subject
(SAE, AE that led to study drug discontinuation - syncope): This was a
27-year-old male s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject received only one dose of study
drug. On Study Day 1, the subject was found to be hypotensive and sent to the emergency
department for evaluation. During evaluation, they had an episode of weakness,
lightheadedness, pallor, tachycardia, and diaphoresis with a depressed mental state but
without syncope while attempting to stand up. The subject was subsequently admitted for the
SAE of syncope and was treated with IV fluids, hydrocodone bitartrate/acetaminophen for
pain, and ondansetron for nausea. On the same day, the SAE of syncope was considered
resolved. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to this SAE. The subject was discharged
from the hospital on Study Day 2.
(b) (6)

9. Subject
(SAE - small intestinal obstruction): This was a 37-year-old female s/p
abdominoplasty surgery. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day
3, the subject was taken by ambulance to a medical center due to complaints of shortness of
breath, nausea, and intractable vomiting. A CT scan of the abdomen and pelvis revealed a
small bowel obstruction. A nasogastric tube was then placed for bowel decompression. On
Study Day 4, the subject was hospitalized for an SAE of small intestinal obstruction. On
Study Day 7, this SAE was considered resolved and the subject was discharged from the
hospital.
(b) (6)

10. Subject
(SAE - infected seroma): This was a 34-year-old female s/p
abdominoplasty surgery. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study Day
15, the subject presented to the emergency department complaining of pain and fever and
was subsequently hospitalized for an SAE of infected seroma. On the same day, the subject
had an AE of moderate wound dehiscence. Treatment included incision and drainage with
wound packing. On Study Day 17, the subject was discharged from the hospital. At the time
of reporting, the SAE of infected seroma and the AE of wound dehiscence were considered
not resolved.
(b) (6)

11. Subject
(SAE - medical device site infection): This was a 20-year-old female
s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject completed treatment with the study drug. On Study
Day 10, the subject presented to the emergency department and was hospitalized for an SAE
of medical device site infection (infection of abdominoplasty drainage tubes). On Study Day
12, the subject was discharged from the hospital and their drains were removed two days
later. At the time of reporting, the SAE of medical device site infection was considered not
resolved.
(b) (6)

12. Subject
(AE that led to study drug discontinuation – hypotension): This was a
42-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. The subject’s relevant medical history
included low baseline blood pressure. The subject received only one dose of study drug. On
the same day (Study Day 1), they had AEs of severe hypotension and severe bradycardia.
Treatment included IV fluids for hypotension and bradycardia. Additional treatment for the
event of bradycardia included placement in the Trendelenburg position, oxygen, and IV

13. Subject
(AE that led to study drug discontinuation - hypotension): This was a
42-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty surgery. On Study Day 1, the subject had AEs of
moderate presyncope and moderate hypotension. They also had an AE of mild oxygen
saturation decreased. Treatment included IV fluids and oxygen. Study drug dosing was
discontinued due to the event of hypotension. On the same day, the AEs of presyncope and
oxygen saturation decreased were considered resolved. On Study Day 2, the AE of
hypotension was considered resolved.
Study VX22-548-107
There were no AEs leading to death. Two (0.8%) subjects had SAEs. Five (2.0%) subjects had
AEs that led to treatment discontinuation. There were no AEs leading to treatment interruption.
(b) (6)

1. Subject
(SAE - suicidal ideation): This was a 42-year-old female s/p
arthroscopic surgery (left ankle) and ligament operation (left ankle ligament repair and
removal of Bassett lesion). On Study Day 8, the subject completed study drug treatment due
to pain resolution. On Study Day 12, they had an SAE of suicidal ideation. The subject felt
that the suicidal ideation was situational, as they were a single parent of multiple children and
had recently ended a relationship. An additional struggle was being nonweight-bearing after
surgery. The subject had already discussed their feelings with their primary care physician
and scheduled therapy. The subject improved over the subsequent weeks and the SAE of
suicidal ideation was considered resolved as of approximately 1 month after the start of this
SAE.
(b) (6)

2. Subject
(SAE - cellulitis): This was a 42-year-old female s/p a skin cosmetic
procedure (brachioplasty). On Study Day 8, the subject completed study drug treatment due
to pain resolution. On Study Day 13, the subject had an AE of moderate cellulitis (right arm).
They were treated with antibiotics in an emergency room and discharged home. On Study
Day 23, the subject was hospitalized for an SAE of cellulitis (worsening right arm cellulitis).
A computerized tomography scan was suggestive of a small abscess within the subcutaneous
tissues of the right upper arm. They were treated with antibiotics and discharged from the
hospital on Study Day 27. Follow-up information received after database lock indicated that
the SAE of cellulitis was considered resolved.
(b) (6)

3. Subject
(AE that led to study drug discontinuation –somnolence): This was a
32-year-old female s/p abdominoplasty and mammoplasty. On Study Day 2, the subject had
an AE of mild somnolence and did not receive treatment for this event. Relevant concomitant
medication before onset of this AE included fluoxetine hydrochloride. On Study Day 4, the
AE of somnolence was considered resolved. Study drug dosing was withdrawn due to this
AE.

4. Subject
(AE that led to study drug discontinuation - accidental overdose): This
was a 50-year-old female who experienced neck pain (acute cervical pain due to fall). On
Study Day 1, the subject had an AE of mild accidental overdose. They took two tablets of
SUZ for 11 succeeding doses from Study Day 1 through Study Day 7, when the overdose
was discovered at their clinic visit and study drug dosing was withdrawn. On Study Day 5,
the subject had an AE of mild dry mouth and did not receive treatment for this event, which
was considered resolved on Study Day 9.
(b) (6)

5. Subject
(AE that led to study drug discontinuation - rash): This was a 56-yearold female who experienced arthralgia (acute left wrist pain). On Study Day 3, the subject
had an AE of mild rash (chest) and, on Study Day 4, they had an AE of mild rash (both
wrists). Treatment included diphenhydramine hydrochloride for both events. Study drug
dosing was withdrawn on Study Day 4 due to these AEs. On Study Day 5, the subject had an
AE of mild pruritus and they continued treatment with diphenhydramine hydrochloride. The
chest rash, pruritis, and wrist rash were considered resolved on Study Day 7, 8, and 9,
respectively.
(b) (6)

6. Subject
(AE that led to study drug discontinuation - arrhythmia): This was a 38year-old female s/p a ligament operation (left anterior cruciate ligament repair). The subject’s
relevant medical history included sinus arrhythmia and cardiac murmur. On Study Day 5,
they experienced exacerbation of preexisting arrhythmia. Study drug dosing was withdrawn
due to this AE. On Study Day 6, ECG results (performed twice) were normal, with the
exception of premature atrial complexes in both readings. At the time of reporting, the
adverse event of arrhythmia was considered not resolved.
(b) (6)

7. Subject
(AE that led to study drug discontinuation - nausea): This was a 65year-old female who experienced post-traumatic pain (right hip pain from fall). The subject’s
relevant medical history included Meniere’s disease, Crohn’s disease, and nausea/vomiting.
On Study Day 2, they had an AE of nausea. The subject stated that they took omeprazole to
help with nausea/vomiting due to Meniere's disease. However, this medication was
prohibited during the study. The subject was not able to tolerate oral intake without
omeprazole and decided to stop taking study drug. On Study Day 3, the AE of nausea was
considered resolved.

18. Clinical Virology
SUZ is not a therapeutic antiviral drug; therefore, clinical virology data were not required or
submitted for this application.

19. Clinical Microbiology
SUZ is not a therapeutic antimicrobial; therefore, clinical microbiology data were not required or
submitted for this application.

4 CONTRAINDICATIONS

5 WARNINGS AND PRECAUTIONS

Rationale for Major Changes to Finalized PI2 Compared to
Applicant’s Draft PI
Based on overall data, the review team’s recommendation for
food effect is, “Avoid consuming food from 2 hours before until
1 hour after the first dose of JOURNAVX to avoid delay in onset
of pain relief. Subsequent doses of JOURNAVX can be taken
with or without food.” Upon further discussion with the
Applicant, the Applicant wanted to add consumption of clear
liquids with the first dose because this was how the efficacy
was conducted. Thus, the following language was agreed upon:
Take the starting dose on an empty stomach at least 1 hour
before or 2 hours after food to avoid delay in onset of action.
Clear liquids may be consumed during this time (e.g., water,
apple juice, vegetable broth, tea, black coffee). Avoid food or
drink containing grapefruit during treatment with JOURNAVX.
There were originally no contraindications listed. The review
team added: “Concomitant use of JOURNAVX with strong
CYP3A4 inhibitors is contraindicated” due to the ≥ 5-fold
exposure that occurs with SUZ in this setting. See the Drug
Interactions section below for further information.
There were originally no warnings and precautions. Four
subsections were added which included: 1) Increased Risk of
Adverse Reactions with Concomitant Use with Strong or
Moderate CYP3A Inhibitors, 2) Risk of Drug Interactions with
Certain CYP3A Substrates, 3) Risk of Drug Interactions with
Certain Hormonal Contraceptives, and 4) Risk of Adverse
Reactions in Patients with Moderate and Severe Hepatic
Impairment.
The rationale for adding these subsections is related to
predicted drug interactions through which serious or otherwise
clinically significant outcomes may occur, and in the case of
hepatic impairment, anticipated adverse reactions. These
decisions were made in accordance with guidance for industry
Warnings and Precautions, Contraindications, and Boxed
Warning Sections of Labeling for Human Prescription Drug and
Biological Products – Content and Format (October 2011).
A warning regarding potential renal impairment was considered
but not included. Although mean changes in CPK and
creatinine from baseline were higher and the mean change in
eGFR from baseline was lower in the SUZ group compared to
placebo, the magnitude of these changes were small and
unlikely to be clinically significant. Notably, the changes from
baseline in these parameters for the SUZ groups were minimal,
while the changes from baseline for the placebo groups
indicated an overall improvement, possibly attributable to
perioperative hydration. Thus, while there is a potential signal
that SUZ could counteract the positive impact of perioperative
hydration, it cannot be confirmed by these perioperative
studies, and ultimately, the observed changes were unlikely to
be clinically significant. No warning regarding renal impairment
was added.

24. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
The following Pediatric Research Equity Act PMRs will be issued at the time of approval.
Moderate to severe acute pain affects pediatric patients and, similar to adults, there is a need for
non-opioid analgesics in this population. However, given the risks inherent to a new
pharmacologic class of medications, the review team favors a conservative approach to the
pediatric studies. The study in the oldest age group (12 to <17 years) will be completed before
proceeding with the subsequent age group (6 to <12 years) to confirm that the safety data is
favorable before exposing younger cohorts. Some overlap, as specified in the Agreed initial
Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP), will be allowed between the studies in younger cohorts. Careful
consideration was given to balancing the safety of the study subjects with the need for
expeditious development of SUZ for use in pediatric patients. Further, the review team
thoroughly discussed the dates below with the DAAP Safety team, acknowledging that the
overlap of dates is intentional. These are effectively interdependent PMRs which will increase
the likelihood that milestone dates may be missed and will need to be re-negotiated.
1. Conduct a randomized, controlled study evaluating the pharmacokinetic, safety, and efficacy
of JOURNAVX in pediatric patients aged 12 to less than 17 years with moderate to severe
acute pain.
Draft protocol submission: 03/2025
Final protocol submission: 09/2025
Study completion: 06/2027
Final report submission: 12/2027
2. Conduct a randomized, controlled study evaluating the pharmacokinetic, safety, and efficacy
of JOURNAVX in pediatric patients aged 6 to less than 12 years with moderate to severe
acute pain.
Draft protocol submission: 09/2027
Final protocol submission: 03/2028
Study completion: 03/2030
Final report submission: 09/2030

The following postmarketing requirements (PMRs) are recommended by the Division of
Pediatrics and Maternal Health (DPMH) and describe a pregnancy exposure registry study,
complementary pregnancy study, and lactation study.
1. Collect global data from prospective pregnancy exposure registry/registries, preferably
disease-based multiproduct pregnancy registry/registries, using a registry-based cohort study
design to compare the maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes of women exposed to Journavx
during pregnancy with unexposed comparator population(s). Align the U.S. study protocol
with protocol(s) outside the United States to reach the target sample size. The registry will
identify and record pregnancy complications, spontaneous abortion, stillbirths, neonatal
deaths, major and minor congenital malformations, pregnancy terminations, preterm births,
small-for-gestational-age births, and any other adverse outcomes, including postnatal growth
and development. These outcomes should be assessed throughout pregnancy. Infant
